<!-- PAGE 11 -->

Innovation. Inclusion. Integrity.

ELI M. REMOLONA, JR. 
Governor

---

<!-- PAGE 12 -->

2025 was a year of shifting narratives.

The  ﬁrst  half  carried  the  momentum  of  2024.  Inﬂation  kept  easing,

hitting  a  record  low  of  0.9  percent  in  July.  That  gave  the  Bangko

Sentral  ng  Pilipinas  (BSP)  room  to  keep  the  key  policy  rate  at

4.5 percent. Growth held close to its strong 2024 pace.

The  second  half  told  a  different  story.  Government  reforms  tied  to

the  ﬂood  control  controversy  slowed  public  infrastructure  spending.

As  uncertainty  spread,  business  and  consumer  sentiment  softened.

Growth  eased  to  4.4  percent  for  the  full  year.  Inﬂation  stayed  below

target, averaging 1.7 percent, which helped support investments.

The good news is that the banking system remained in robust health.

Banks were well-capitalized and were ﬂush with liquidity. The banking

system continued to support lending and economic activity.

That  resilience  was  no  accident.  Years  of  prudent  supervision  and

regulation  ensured  that  our  banks  were  in  a  good  position  to  absorb

shocks.

Even  as  conditions  grew more  challenging,  the  BSP  pressed  forward.

Alongside our core mandates, the BSP continued to pursue initiatives

that reﬂect our commitment to innovation, inclusion, and integrity.

ii | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 13 -->

Innovation

Good  policy  needs  good  and  timely  information.  In  2025,  the  BSP

stepped up its use of artiﬁcial intelligence (AI) to better track a rapidly

changing  environment.  We  employed  large  language  models  to

measure  sentiment  and  monitor  trade  ﬂows.  These  allowed  us  to

formulate  a  more  data-driven  monetary  policy  that  is  ahead  of  the

curve.

In  payments,  the  BSP  advanced  the  adoption  of  ISO  20022.

This is a global standard that makes both domestic and cross-border 
transactions  more  transparent  and  secure.  We  also  made  progress 
in cross-border payments through the incorporation of Nexus Global

Payments  in  Singapore.  Once  fully  operational,  Project  Nexus  will

link instant payment systems of member countries. This will enable

seamless cross-border transactions.

We  also  strengthened  cybersecurity.  The  BSP  developed  and 
tested  the  Cybersecurity  Incident  Response  Plan  for  PhilPaSSplus,  
our  real-time  gross  settlement  system.  During  the  year,  the  system

processed  a  record  ₱601.3  trillion  in  transactions  at  a  99.9  percent

efﬁciency  rate.  Meanwhile,  transactions  on  InstaPay,  the  low-value

instant payment system, surged to 4.7 billion worth ₱11.6 trillion.

2025 BSP Annual Report I iii

---

<!-- PAGE 14 -->

Inclusion

More  Filipinos  now  have  access  to  ﬁnancial  services  than  ever  before.

Account  ownership  among  Filipino  households

increased  to

85.0  percent.  Beyond  gaining  access  to  digital  ﬁnancial  services,

Filipinos also became more conﬁdent in using them in daily life.

The  Paleng-QR  Ph  Plus  Program  expanded  to  more  than  900  local

government  units.  This  brought  the  beneﬁts  of  digital  payments  to

market vendors, transport drivers, microentrepreneurs, and informal

workers across the country.

But access is only a starting point. The BSP elevated ﬁnancial health 
as a Priority Economic Deliverable (PED) for the 2026 ASEAN Summit,

which is chaired by the Philippines.

Integrity

Integrity  remained  the  foundation  of  trust  in  the  ﬁnancial  system.

In  February,  the  Philippines  exited  the  Financial  Action  Task  Force’s

anti-money  laundering  grey  list.  This  made  it  easier  and  cheaper

for  businesses  and  individuals,  especially  overseas  Filipino  workers,

to  remit  funds.  We  also  exited  the  high-risk  jurisdiction  lists  of  the

European Union and the United Kingdom.

In  September,  to  further  strengthen  the  integrity  of  the  ﬁnancial

system,  the  BSP  set  new  rules  for  withdrawals.  The  rules  are

designed to curb money laundering and other criminal activity while

minimizing  impact  on  businesses  and  individuals  with  legitimate

and  regular  transactions.  We  supported  this  by  strengthening  the

standards for external auditors and raising reporting requirements for

non-bank ﬁnancial institutions such as pawnshops and money service

businesses.

iv | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 15 -->

In August, responding to growing public concern, the BSP required

banks, electronic wallet operators, and other BSP-supervised ﬁnancial

institutions to remove links to online gambling sites.

With  these  measures,  we  ensure  that  as  ﬁnancial  services  become

more  accessible  and  innovative,  the  system  remains  credible  and

worthy of public trust.

Looking ahead

The  economy  is  now  navigating  new  headwinds.  The  Middle  East 
conﬂict early in 2026 pushed inﬂation higher, prompting the BSP to

raise rates.

Fortunately,  we  remain  on  solid  footing.  Inﬂation  is  rising  from  a

low base. Our banks also remain strong, ready to absorb shocks and

sustain lending.

None  of  this  was  achieved  alone.  Our  stakeholders  and  partner

agencies all played their part. So did the Monetary Board and every

BSP ofﬁcer and staff.

Our combined efforts put us in a good position to weather the current

challenges.  I  am  conﬁdent  that  continued  collaboration  will  enable

us to navigate these conditions and further strengthen the Philippine

economy and the ﬁnancial system for the beneﬁt of all Filipinos.

2025 BSP Annual Report I v

<!-- PAGE 16 -->

WHO WE ARE
About the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
The BSP is the country’s independent central monetary authority mandated to promote 
price  stability,  help  maintain  stability  of  the  financial  system,  and  oversee  the  country’s 
payments and settlements system.

Section 20, Article XII, 1987 Philippine Constitution

The  Congress  shall  establish  an  independent  central  monetary  authority…  
[which] shall provide policy direction in the areas of money, banking, and credit.  
It shall have supervision over the operations of banks and exercise such regulatory 
powers as may be provided by law over the operations of finance companies and 
other institutions performing similar functions.

Section 1, Article 1, Chapter 1, Republic Act No. 7653   
(The New Central Bank Act) as amended by Republic Act No. 11211

The  State  shall  maintain  a  central  monetary  authority  that  shall  function  and 
operate  as  an  independent  and  accountable  body  corporate  in  the  discharge 
of  its  mandated  responsibilities  concerning  money,  banking,  and  credit.  In  line 
with this policy, and considering its unique functions and responsibilities, the central 
monetary authority established under this Act,  while  being  a  government-owned 
corporation, shall enjoy fiscal and administrative autonomy.

vi | 2025 BSP Annual Report

The BSP’s Legal Mandate

Legal Basis of Establishment and Legal Mandate

The BSP is the central bank of the Republic of the Philippines. Section 20, 
Article  XII  of  the  1987  Constitution  mandated  Congress  to  establish  an 
independent central monetary authority tasked to provide policy direction 
in the areas of money, banking, and credit. Pursuant to this constitutionally 
mandated  responsibility,  Congress  established  the  BSP  through  the 
enactment of Republic Act No. 7653 in 1993.

Powers and Functions

As  the  Philippines’  central  monetary  authority,  the  BSP  is  responsible,  
among others, for:

•  maintaining  price  stability  conducive  to  a  balanced  and  sustainable

growth of the economy and employment;

•  promoting and maintaining monetary stability and the convertibility of

the peso;

•  promoting  financial  stability  by  closely  working  with  the  national 
government, the Department of Finance, the Securities and Exchange 
Commission,  the  Insurance  Commission,  and  the  Philippine  Deposit 
Insurance Corporation;

•  overseeing  the  payments  and  settlements  system,

including 
infrastructures,  to  promote  sound  and

critical  financial  market 
prudent practices; and

•  promoting  broad  and  convenient  access  to  high  quality  financial

services for the interest of the general public.

viii I 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 19 -->

The BSP is also tasked to perform the following functions:

Liquidity management.  The BSP formulates and implement monetary policy 
aimed  at  influencing  money  supply,  consistent  with  its  primary  mandate  of 
maintaining price stability.

Currency issue. The BSP has the exclusive power to issue the national currency.  
All  notes  and  coins  issued  by  the  BSP  are  fully  guaranteed  by  the 
government  and  are  considered 
for  all  private  and  
public debts.

legal  tender

Lender  of  last  resort.  The  BSP  extends  discounts,  loans,  and  advances  to 
banking institutions for liquidity purposes.

Financial supervision. The BSP supervises banks and exercises regulatory and 
examination  powers  over  non-bank  institutions  performing  quasi-banking 
functions, money service businesses, credit granting businesses, and payment 
system operators.

Management  of  foreign  currency  reserves.  The  BSP  seeks  to  maintain 
sufficient  international  reserves  to  meet  any  foreseeable  net  demands  for 
foreign currencies to preserve the international stability and convertibility of 
the Philippine peso.

Determination  of  exchange  rate  policy.  The  BSP  determines  the  exchange 
rate policy of the Philippines. Currently, it adheres to a market-oriented foreign 
exhange rate policy to ensure orderly conditions in the market.

Bond issue. As part of monetary policy, the BSP may issue, place, buy, and sell 
freely negotiable evidences of indebtedness.

the  government.  The  BSP

the  banker, 
Banker  of 
financial  advisor,  and  official  depository  of  the  government,  its  political 
subdivisions  and  instrumentalities,  and  government-owned  and  -controlled

functions  as

In 2019, over 25 years after the BSP was established, Republic Act No. 11211 was signed to 
amend  the  BSP  Charter.  This  law  implemented  reforms  that  align  the  BSP  with  global 
standards,  improve  its  operations,  and  enhance  its  policy-making  that  responds  to 
financial markets and the economy.

The supervisory and regulatory powers of the BSP are further enhanced by expanding the 
coverage of institutions under the Bank’s supervision to include money service businesses, 
credit granting businesses, and payment system operators.

2025 BSP Annual Report I ix

<!-- PAGE 22 -->

From strategic direction  
to disciplined execution: 
Orchestrating  
the BSP 2024–2029 
Enterprise Strategy

Following the launch of the BSP Enterprise Strategy in 2024, the Bank’s priority in 2025 was 
clear: translate strategic direction into disciplined, coordinated execution across all operating 
units. This required converting strategic priorities into measurable, shared commitments; 
maintaining organizational alignment; and establishing systematic review mechanisms to 
track progress and address emerging gaps.

Effective  strategy  orchestration  keeps  the  organization  moving  as  one.  It  strengthens 
accountability,  supports  timely  decision-making,  and  advances  the  BSP’s  objectives  of 
fostering  innovation,  promoting  financial  inclusion,  and  upholding  institutional  integrity.

xii I 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 23 -->

Turning strategy 
into coordinated action

Strategy  execution  begins  with  effective  cascade.  The  BSP  Enterprise  Strategy  is 
translated into clear priorities, measurable outcomes, and adequately resourced initiatives.  
This  enables  every  sector  and  office  to  align  work  programs  with  institution-wide  goals. 
Such alignment ensures that interdependent initiatives move  together and performance 
targets remain consistent across units.

In 2025, the BSP strengthened this “line of sight” from enterprise priorities to sectoral plans 
and  individual  unit  contributions.  The  Bank  enhanced  linkages  between  the  enterprise 
strategy and sectoral operational plans, supported operating units throughout the planning 
cycle, and reinforced governance mechanisms for reviewing priorities, resource allocation, 
and results. The highlights of the year include:

adopting  a  common  planning  framework  across  sectors, 
improving clarity of priorities and accountability for results;

aligning Annual Work Programs with enterprise and sectoral 
priorities,  strengthening  links  between  plans,  budgets,  and 
expected results

enhancing  strategy  governance  through  clearer  guidelines 
and  advisories,  standardizing  planning,  review,  prioritization, 
and monitoring processes; and

deepening  strategy  communication  and  engagement, 
equipping execution teams with a common understanding to 
support consistent implementation across the Bank.

2025 BSP Annual Report I xiii

---

<!-- PAGE 24 -->

Strengthening strategy delivery 
through project management 
discipline

Project  management  discipline  is  fundamental  to  strategy  delivery.  It  provides  structure 
to complex, interdependent work by clarifying accountabilities, identifying risks early, and 
aligning project delivery with intended outcomes.

In  2025,  the  BSP  strengthened  project  discipline  by  developing  new  Guidelines  on 
Post-Implementation  Review  and  Project  Quality  Assurance.  These  guidelines  set  clearer 
standards  for  assessing  project  outcomes  and  ensuring  compliance  with  established 
processes. Management oversight was reinforced through enhanced Project Status Reports, 
which improved visibility of milestones, risks, and critical dependencies.

To  build  institutional  capability,  the  BSP  expanded  engagement  across  project  teams 
through  the  Enterprise  Project  Management  Roadshow.  The  Bank  also  strengthened 
peer  learning  through  the  Project  Managers  Community  of  Practice  and  Interests  Circle.  
These  platforms  supported  knowledge  sharing,  coordination,  and  problem-solving 
across  teams.  With  stronger  standards,  improved  reporting,  and  better  collaboration,  
the BSP advanced a more coherent, institution-wide approach to project delivery in support 
of its enterprise strategy.

Keeping strategy on track

Strategy  remains  relevant  only  through  active  management.  This  requires  consistent 
monitoring, structured reviews, and timely course correction.

In  2025,  the  BSP  strengthened  enterprise-wide  strategy  monitoring  through  a  more 
structured cadence of engagements and formal review forums. These platforms validated 
performance,  identified  bottlenecks  and  emerging  risks,  and  confirmed  corrective 
actions—ensuring  that  performance  discussions  remained  timely,  evidence-based,  and 
results-oriented.

Reporting  standards  were  reinforced  through  improved  validation  of  performance 
submissions from Strategy Execution Teams (SETs). Consolidated insights were presented 
in  the  Annual  and  Mid-Year  Strategy  Performance  Reports  for  the  Monetary  Board  (MB), 
providing clear, decision-ready information. To maintain strategic oversight, regular briefings 
between  the  MB  and  SETs  were  also  conducted,  deepening  leadership  alignment  and 
sustaining execution momentum.

Together,  these  enhanced  monitoring  frameworks  and  clearer  feedback  mechanisms 
position the BSP to maintain strategic focus and deliver measurable outcomes across the 
institution.

xiv I 2025 BSP Annual Report

<!-- PAGE 25 -->

Driving performance and 
measurable outcomes

What  gets  measured  gets  improved.  Monitoring  performance  allows  the  BSP  to  assess 
what works, identify emerging gaps, and reinforce accountability.

In  2025,  the  BSP  made  steady  progress  in  its  strategic  objectives.  Monetary  policy 
formulation  improved  through  stronger  use  of  technology,  research,  and  analytical  tools. 
Initiatives  to  strengthen  systemic  risk  oversight  and  crisis  management  progressed  as 
planned.  Capital  market  reforms  gained  traction,  supported  by  preparatory  work  for 
the  possible  inclusion  of  Philippine  bonds  in  the  J.P.  Morgan  index.  Sustainability  efforts 
advanced through the establishment of dedicated environmental, social, and governance 
offices  and  the  integration  of  climate  considerations  into  policy  analysis.  Research  and 
collaboration expanded and exceeded targets, resulting in more timely and evidence-based  
decision-making.

The  BSP  also  strengthened  its  institutional  foundations.  The  rollout  of  the  enhanced 
Enterprise Human Resource System (EHRS) improved key processes and generated early 
efficiency gains. Data management and cybersecurity enhancements progressed alongside 
the  automation  of  regulatory  workflows  and  pilot  testing  of  artificial  intelligence  (AI) 
tools. Stakeholder engagement also improved through the expansion of digital platforms  
and strategic partnerships.

Looking  ahead,  the  BSP  will  focus  on  accelerating  key  deliverables  and  strengthening 
critical  enablers—particularly 
infrastructure,  performance  management,  
and talent development—to sustain progress and translate plans into measurable outcomes. 
The  Bank  remains  committed  to  fostering  a  culture  of  collaboration,  accountability,  
and innovation in advancing its strategic objectives.

Strategic objectives

SO1

Enhanced 
monetary policy 
framework

The BSP strengthens the monetary policy framework by advancing 
research  capabilities,  improving  macroeconomic  models  and  data 
sets,  and  leveraging  modern  technologies  for  data-driven  analysis, 
communication,  and  policy  coordination.  Key  accomplishments 
include:

•

conducting  research  studies  using  both  structured  and  
unstructured  data  to  assess  monetary  policy  frameworks,  
transmission, communication, and inflation expectations and  
dynamics, among others; and

•  generating models and indicators from big data and machine  
learning,  such  as  (a)  the  PH  Non-Farm  Payroll  Index,  which  
provides a snapshot of local employment  trends; and (b) the  
C-TRENDS model, which nowcasts household consumption on 
a monthly basis.

The BSP strengthens its ability to detect and manage risks that could 
affect the financial system. By improving oversight, supervision, and 
crisis management, the Bank can respond more quickly and effectively 
to  potential  threats.  A  strong  risk  monitoring  and  communication 
system helps the BSP assess and address both local and global risks, 
ensuring stability and confidence in the banking system.

•  developing an inventory of systemic risk surveillance tools; and

•  delivering  initial  outputs  for  the  BSP  Lender  of  Last  Resort  
  Operational Framework.

Deepened  
capital markets

The BSP contributes to the deepening and broadening of Philippine 
capital  markets  to  support  sustainable  economic  growth  through 
stronger monetary transmission, robust financial benchmarks, a more 
accessible corporate bond market, and a robust regulatory environment 
for emerging financial technologies. Key accomplishments include:

•  transitioning the BSP’s overnight reverse repurchase facility to  
  a Global Master Repurchase Agreement-based facility;

• 
implementing  reforms—including  coordination  and  collaboration  
  with  the  Bureau  of  the  Treasury  on  streamlining  the  tax  treaty  
  procedure, ongoing yield curve consolidation, and enhancement of  
the primary dealer system—that led to the Philippines being placed  
  under  final  review  for  inclusion  in  the  J.P.  Morgan  Government  
  Bond  Index–Emerging  Markets  Global  Diversified  Index,  which  
  would give the BSP a 1.27 percent allocation in the portfolio; and

•  engaging  with  the  Bankers  Association  of  the  Philippines  on  
  expanding the interbank repo market and launching the enhanced  
  PHP interest rate swap market.

The  BSP  integrates  environmental  and  climate  considerations  into 
financial sector policies, promotes financing for adaptation and green 
transition, and fosters a sustainability-driven culture in its operations. 
Key accomplishments include:

•  amending  the  mandatory  disclosures  on  climate-related

financial risks; and

•  updating the Sustainable Finance Taxonomy Guidelines.

How we were in 2025: The global and domestic 
macroeconomic landscape

Global economic developments

Global growth proved resilient in 2025 despite elevated trade tensions and policy 
uncertainty.  Favorable  ﬁnancial  conditions  and  strong  technology-related  trade 
supported  activity,  offsetting  the  drag  from  higher  United  States  (US)  tariffs.1 
The International Monetary Fund (IMF) estimated global gross domestic product 
(GDP) growth at 3.3 percent, while the World Bank (WB) placed it at 2.7 percent. 
Growth  was  supported  by  inventory  buildup,  strong  investor  risk  appetite,  and 
rising artiﬁcial intelligence (AI) investments.2

Global  ﬁnancial  markets  reﬂected  this  resilience.  The  Bank  for  International 
Settlements  (BIS)  observed  compressed  credit  spreads  and  elevated  equity 
valuations, particularly among large technology ﬁrms. These conditions signaled a 
strong risk appetite but also increased the risk of abrupt market corrections.3

<!-- PAGE 31 -->

The year began with expectations of a soft landing. Inﬂation was still above-target  
in  most  economies,  but  labor  markets  normalized,  and  ﬁnancial  conditions 
remained  accommodative.  However,  broad-based  US  tariff  increases  in  Q2 2025 
triggered a sharp reassessment of risk. Equity markets declined, sovereign yields 
became volatile, and the US dollar initially weakened. Fiscal concerns and doubts 
over central bank independence ampliﬁed market reactions.

Although trade tensions partially eased in the second half of the year, uncertainty 
persisted.  Global  ﬁnancial  conditions  remained  broadly  supportive,  but  asset 
performance  diverged.  Sovereign  yields  were  mixed  across  markets,  while  most 
equity 
large  technology  ﬁrms  continued  to 
outperform, underscoring the concentration of stock market gains.

indices  recovered.  Meanwhile,

Global  activity  held  up  amid  easing  trade  tensions  and  still-favorable  ﬁnancing 
conditions. The IMF estimates that global growth slowed to 2.4 percent in Q3 2025, 
as  stronger-than-expected  performance  in  some  economies  offset  weaknesses  
in  others.  Technology-related  trade  continued  to  support  overall  trade  volumes, 
mitigating the impact of softer demand in other sectors.

Looking  ahead,  global  growth  is  expected  to  remain  broadly  stable.  The  IMF 
projects  global  output  to  expand  by  3.3 percent  in  2026  and  3.2 percent  in  2027. 
The WB  projects  growth  at  2.6–2.7 percent  over  the  same  period.  Advanced 
economies (AEs) are expected to expand modestly amid structural constraints and 
moderating  demand.4  Emerging  market  and  developing  economies  are  seen  to 
continue outpacing advanced peers, although elevated debt burdens and tighter 
external ﬁnancing conditions constrain several low-income economies.5

Risks  to  the  global  outlook  for  2026  and  2027  remain  tilted  to  the  downside. 
Asset valuations  and  investments  appear  sensitive  to  shifts  in  expectations 
surrounding AI-driven productivity gains. Renewed trade or geopolitical tensions 
could  tighten  ﬁnancial  conditions,  disrupt  supply  chains,  raise  inﬂation,  and 
weaken trade ﬂows. At the same time, faster technological diffusion and sustained 
policy coordination could lift potential growth.

4  The  IMF  forecasts  average  growth  in  AEs  at  1.8 percent  in  2026  and  1.7 percent  in  2027.  In  the  US,  output  is 
projected  to  grow  by  2.4 percent  in  2026,  supported  by  ﬁscal  spending  and  a  less  restrictive  monetary  stance, 
before easing to 2.0 percent in 2027. Growth in the euro area is seen at 1.3 percent in 2026 and 1.4 percent in 2027, 
aided by higher public spending in select states despite persistent structural constraints. 
5 The IMF expects output to rise slightly above 4.0 percent in 2026–2027, while WB estimates indicate a gradual 
easing  from  4.2 percent  in  2025  to  around  4.0–4.1 percent.  China  and  India  are  expected  to  remain  key  growth 
drivers,  while  many  low-income  and  frontier  markets  face  weaker  prospects  due  to  elevated  debt  and  tighter 
external ﬁnancing conditions.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 3

<!-- PAGE 32 -->

Philippine economic developments

Growth softens amid weaker domestic demand.

The Philippine economy grew by 4.4 percent in 2025, slower than the 5.7-percent 
growth  in  2024.  Weaker  outturns  in  the  second  half  of  the  year  weighed  on 
full-year performance.  Domestic  demand  softened  notably  due  to  a  contraction  
in  capital  formation,  particularly  in  public  construction,  and  more  moderate 
household consumption. On the supply side, growth in the industry and services 
sector decelerated.

Compared  with  regional  peers,  the  Philippines  posted  relatively  weaker  growth 
than  Vietnam  (8.0 percent),  Malaysia  (5.2 percent),  Indonesia  (5.1 percent),  China 
(5.0 percent), and Singapore (5.0 percent), but outpaced Thailand (2.4 percent).

Gross  national  income  increased  by  6.1 percent,  moderating  from  7.7 percent  
in the previous year. This was primarily due to slower growth in net primary income, 
which eased from 26.1 percent to 19.7 percent.

4 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 33 -->

Aggregate output and demand

Supply side

On  the  production  side,  the  slowdown  in  growth  was  driven  by  weaker 
performance in the industry and services sectors, partly offset by a rebound in the 
agriculture,  ﬁshery,  and  forestry  (AFF)  sector.  Industry  growth,  in  particular,  was 
weighed  down  by  weaker  construction  and  manufacturing  activity,  reﬂecting 
delays in government construction projects and subdued demand conditions.

The industry sector grew by 1.7 percent in 2025, marking its weakest performance 
since  2020.  It  contributed  0.5 percentage  point  (ppt)  to  real  GDP  growth  and 
accounted  for  28.3 percent  of  total  output.  The  slowdown  was  largely  driven  by 
weaker construction activity, following the ongoing infrastructure probe into ﬂood 
control projects.

Manufacturing growth also moderated due to weak demand and cost pressures,6 
with  output  declines  observed  in  the  chemical  and  petroleum  industries. 
Meanwhile, mining and quarrying posted a modest recovery, supported by higher 
coal production.

Figure 3 
Gross Domestic Product by Industry 
at constant 2018 prices; annual growth rate in percent

Source: Philippine Statistics Authority

6 This was reported in the S&P Global and Philippine Institute for Supply Management’s Purchasing Managers’ 
Index (PMI) for Philippine manufacturing.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 5

---

<!-- PAGE 34 -->

The  services  sector  remained  the  primary  driver  of  domestic  economic  growth 
in 2025.  It  expanded  by  5.8 percent,  easing  from  6.7 percent  in  2024.  Despite  the 
slowdown, the sector contributed 3.7 ppts to real GDP growth and accounted for 
63.8 percent of total output.

However,  growth  in  the  services  sector  moderated,  reﬂecting  slower  expansion 
across  key  subsectors.  Financial  and  insurance  activities  decelerated,  as  banks 
adopted  a  more  cautious  lending  stance  amid  weaker  investor  sentiment, 
particularly in H2 2025.7

Professional  and  business  services  growth  also  softened,  as  global  headwinds 
weighed  on  outsourcing  demand  and  tempered  expansion  in  the  information 
technology and business process management (IT-BPM) industry.8 Tourism-related 
activities likewise slowed, with softer growth in accommodation and food services, 
as well as transportation and storage, amid subdued tourist arrivals.9

These  slowdowns  were  partly  offset  by  stronger  growth  in  public  administration 
and defense, education, and human health and social work activities.

The  AFF  sector  expanded  by  3.1 percent  in  2025,  recovering  from  a  1.5-percent 
contraction  in  the  previous  year.  It  contributed  0.2 ppt  to  overall  GDP  growth  
and  accounted  for  7.9 percent  of  total  output.  The  rebound  was  primarily  driven  
by  higher  crop  production,  particularly  palay,  corn,  and  sugarcane.  This  was 
supported  by  more  favorable  post-El  Niño  and  La  Niña  growing  conditions  and 
improved farming practices. Livestock production also recovered, reﬂecting a lower 
incidence of African Swine Fever and stronger biosecurity measures.

Demand side

On  the  expenditure  side,  growth  was  dampened  by  a  decline  in  investment  
and  a  moderation  in  household  consumption.  This  was  partly  offset  by  stronger 
government spending and a rebound in net exports (3.0 percent).

Household ﬁnal consumption expenditure (HFCE) eased from 4.9 percent in 2024 
to  4.5 percent  in  2025,  reﬂecting  subdued  consumer  sentiment  and  the  adverse 
effects  of  weather  disturbances.  Despite  the  slowdown,  household  spending 
remained  the  main  driver  of  growth,  contributing  3.3 ppts  to  real  GDP  growth  
and accounting for 72.6 percent of total output.

7 Aligned with the slowdown in manufacturing (-9.4 percent) and other services (-5.8 percent), as well as the contraction 
in construction (-5.4 percent), outstanding production loans in universal and commercial banks declined in 2025.

the 7.0-percent increase recorded in the previous year.

https://www.philstar.com/business/2026/01/29/2504140/it-bpm-revenues-breach-40-billion-mark

---

<!-- PAGE 35 -->

Spending patterns varied across categories. HFCE growth was supported mainly 
by  higher  spending  on  essential  items,10,11  particularly  food  and  non-alcoholic 
beverages,  as  well  as  education.  In  contrast,  other  expenditure  categories 
decelerated relative to the previous year.

Overall, household consumption was weighed down by weather-related disruptions 
that  affected  daily  activities  and  travel,  along  with  weaker  consumer  sentiment 
amid governance concerns in H2 2025.12,13 Consumer sentiment was slightly more 
pessimistic (-14.7 percent) than the previous year (-14.5 percent).14

Gross  capital  formation  contracted  by  1.7 percent,  largely  due  to  a  sharp  fall  in 
general  government  construction,  which  reduced  overall  GDP  growth  by  1.0 ppt. 
The downturn reﬂected the ban on new infrastructure projects during the midterm 
election period, compounded by stricter validation procedures for Department of 
Public  Works  and  Highways  (DPWH)  civil  works  and  project  delays  caused  by 
typhoons and earthquakes in H2 2025.15 Inventory drawdowns also contributed to 
the  contraction,  following  frontloaded  stock  accumulation  in  2024  and  amid 
heightened external uncertainty in 2025.

Government ﬁnal consumption expenditure grew by 8.4 percent and contributed 
1.2 ppts to GDP growth. This was driven by base effects, election-related spending, 
and salary adjustments.

Net  exports  improved  in  2025,  contributing  0.3 ppt  to  GDP  growth,  as  export 
growth outpaced imports. Total exports and imports expanded by 8.2 percent and 
5.0 percent, respectively.

Merchandise exports recorded a strong recovery, growing by 14.9 percent in 2025 
after a 0.6-percent contraction in 2024. The rebound was driven mainly by electronics, 
particularly  semiconductors,16  amid  ﬁrmer  global  demand.  This  was  partly  offset  
by slower growth in services exports, reﬂecting a moderation in business process 
outsourcing (BPO) and tourism.

10 Essential items include food and non-alcoholic beverages; housing, water, electricity, gas, and other fuels; health;

Merchandise imports accelerated from 2.0 percent in 2024 to 4.7 percent in 2025. 
This  was  largely  due  to  increased  imports  of  electronic  components  and 
semiconductor-related inputs, consistent with stronger export-oriented production. 
Meanwhile,  growth  in  services  imports  slowed  from  11.9 percent  to  6.1 percent, 
reﬂecting contractions in transport and other miscellaneous services.

Medium-term outlook

Over  the  medium  term,  growth  is  expected  to  remain  anchored  on  sound 
macroeconomic fundamentals despite persistent domestic and global headwinds. 
External risks, particularly from heightened geopolitical tensions in the Middle East 
and  evolving  global  trade  policies,  may  continue  to  exert  upward  pressure  on 
commodity prices and disrupt supply chains. Domestically, unresolved governance 
concerns could temper business and consumer confidence, potentially constraining 
investment  and  demand.  Nonetheless,  sustained  macroeconomic  stability  and 
ongoing reforms are expected to provide underlying support to growth.

On the demand side, steady remittance inflows are expected to continue supporting 
household  consumption.  Meanwhile,  continued  structural  and  governance 
reforms may help improve the business climate, support investment activity, and 
enhance private sector participation, particularly in infrastructure development.17

17 Major reforms enacted in 2025 include the Government Optimization Act, the Open Access in Data Transmission 
(Konektadong Pinoy) Act, the Enhanced Fiscal Regime for Large-Scale Metallic Mining Act, the E-Governance Act, 
the Accelerated Reformed Right-of-Way Act, the Philippine National Nuclear Energy Safety Act, and amendments 
to the Investors’ Lease Act.

8 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 37 -->

Meanwhile,  the  country’s  external  position  is  expected  to  remain  favorable, 
underpinned by efforts to improve trade facilitation, expand market access through 
free trade agreements, and revitalize tourism. Tourism activity is expected to improve 
modestly with deeper regional connectivity; eased visa requirements, particularly 
for Chinese and Indian nationals; and the launch of a Digital Nomad Visa. Sustained 
investment  in  accommodation  capacity  and  ongoing  airport  upgrades  are  also 
expected to support the sector’s medium-term resilience.

On  the  supply  side,  agricultural  output  is  expected  to  beneﬁt  from  sustained 
investments in climate resilience, continued assistance to disaster-affected areas, 
and measures to enhance productivity and competitiveness. In the industry sector, 
improvements  in  the  tax  regime  and  ease  of  doing  business—particularly  in 
mining, automotive, and semiconductor manufacturing—are expected to support 
production  and  investment.  Meanwhile,  growth  in  the  services  sector  could  be 
sustained  by  continued  improvements  in  digital  connectivity  and  ongoing 
investments in workforce development.

Labor and employment conditions

Labor market conditions slightly soften, particularly in the latter half of 2025.

Total  employment  rose  by  172,000  to  49.0 million.  However,  labor  force  growth 
lower  average  employment  rate  of

outpaced 
95.8 percent, down by 0.4 ppt from 2024.

job  creation,  resulting

The  services  sector  continued  to  absorb  the  majority  of  workers,  accounting  for 
61.7 percent of total employment. This was followed by agriculture at 20.5 percent 
and industry at 17.8 percent.

Employment  gains  were  concentrated  in  administrative  and  support  service 
activities  (+148,000),  accommodation  and  food  service  activities  (+143,000),  and 
ﬁshing  and  aquaculture  (+109,000).  These 
increases  were  partly  offset  by 
employment  losses  in  manufacturing  (-177,000)  and  agriculture  and  forestry 
(-124,000),  likely  reﬂecting  softer  demand  and  weather-related  disruptions  in  the 
latter part of the year.18

The  share  of  wage  and  salary  workers  remained  unchanged,  accounting  for 
63.9 percent  of  total  employment.  The  share  of  unpaid  family  workers  rose  to 
6.7 percent  (from  6.6 percent),  while  self-employed  individuals  without  paid 
employees remained steady at 27.5 percent. Meanwhile, employers in family-operated 
farms or businesses declined from 2.1 percent to 1.9 percent.

The unemployment rate rose from 3.8 percent to 4.2 percent, equivalent to around 
200,000  additional  unemployed  Filipinos.  This  partly  reﬂected  faster  labor  force 
expansion  alongside  weaker  employment  creation  in  key  sectors  such  as 
manufacturing  and  agriculture.  Nevertheless,  the  outturn  remained  below  the 
government’s  4.8–5.1 percent  target  under  the  Philippine  Development  Plan  
2023–2028 Midterm Update.

18  The  country  experienced  17  typhoons  in  Q3  and  Q4  2025,  higher  than  the  11  typhoons  recorded  in  the  same 
period in 2024.

<!-- PAGE 38 -->

Males  accounted  for  a  larger  share  of  the  unemployed  (56.9 percent).  College 
graduates comprised 35.5 percent of the total unemployed persons. By age group, 
individuals  aged  25–34  accounted  for  the  largest  share  (36.7 percent),  exceeding 
the youth cohort aged 15–24 (34.9 percent).

The number of underemployed persons increased marginally by 5,000, while the 
underemployment  rate  remained  unchanged  at  11.9 percent.  The  rise  in  visible 
underemployment  was  partly  offset  by  a  decline  in  invisible  underemployment. 
Most underemployed workers were in the services sector (50.8 percent), followed 
by agriculture (30.5 percent) and industry (18.7 percent).

Meanwhile,  the  labor  force  participation  rate  (LFPR)  edged  down  by  0.4 ppt  to 
64.1 percent in 2025.19 Male LFPR averaged 74.6 percent, while female LFPR stood 
at 53.4 percent.

Overall,  labor  market  conditions  remained  broadly  stable  but  showed  signs  of 
softening amid weaker domestic demand.

Prices

Inﬂation falls below the target in 2025.

Headline inﬂation averaged 1.7 percent in 2025, below the government’s target of 
3.0 percent and the tolerance range ± 1.0 ppt around the target.

Food  inﬂation  eased  during  the  year,  mainly  due  to  lower  rice  prices.  Improved 
domestic  production  and  lower  tariff  rates  helped  moderate  domestic  retail  
rice  prices.  International  rice  prices  declined  after  India  and  Pakistan  lifted  their 
export restrictions.

Non-food inflation also slowed in 2025. Inflation for restaurants and accommodation 
services moderated, while transport inﬂation declined due to rollbacks in domestic 
gasoline and diesel pump prices.

Across regions, headline inﬂation decelerated in both the National Capital Region 
(NCR)  and  areas  outside  NCR  (AONCR).  Inﬂation  in  NCR  eased  from  2.6 percent  
in  2024  to  2.4 percent  in  2025.  In  AONCR,  inﬂation  slowed  more  sharply  from 
3.4 percent to 1.5 percent.

Core  inﬂation  declined,  reaching  2.4 percent  in  2025  from  3.0 percent  in  the 
previous  year.  Alternative  measures  of  core  inflation  estimated  by  the  BSP,  
such as the trimmed mean and weighted median, also moderated relative to the 
previous year.

Monetary aggregates

Domestic liquidity remains supportive of economic activity.

Domestic liquidity (M3) grew by 7.2 percent year-on-year (y-o-y) as of end-December 
2025,20  following  a  7.7-percent  expansion  in  the  previous  year.  Rising  claims  on  
the  domestic  sector  continued  to  drive  M3  growth,  reﬂecting  the  impact  of  the 
BSP’s  monetary  policy  easing.  Correspondingly,  liquidity  in  the  ﬁnancial  system 
remained ample to support economic activity.

Domestic claims increased by 10.5 percent y-o-y in December 2025, slightly higher 
than  10.4 percent  in  December 2024.  Claims  on  the  private  sector  alone  grew  
by  10.7 percent,  indicating  sustained  bank  lending  to  non-ﬁnancial  ﬁrms  and 
households. Similarly, net claims on the central government rose by 10.8 percent 
amid higher national government (NG) borrowings.

Meanwhile,  net  foreign  assets  (NFA)  in  peso  terms  rose  by  5.9 percent  y-o-y  in 
December 2025,  slightly  lower  than  6.0 percent  a  year  ago.  The  BSP’s  NFA

of foreign currency-denominated debt securities.

Operations of the national government

The ﬁscal gap widens.

In  2025,  the  NG  posted  a  ﬁscal  deﬁcit  of  ₱1.58 trillion,  up  by  4.7 percent  from 
₱1.51 trillion in 2024.21 However, the deﬁcit was only marginally above the full-year 
program of ₱1.56 trillion by 1.0 percent, reflecting slower-than-programmed revenue 
growth.  Moderated  spending,  following  stricter  oversight  and  due  diligence  
in  government  contracts,  helped  offset  weaker  revenues.  As  a  result,  the  ﬁscal  
deficit-to-GDP  ratio  eased  from  5.7  percent  in  2024  to  5.6  percent  in  2025,  
broadly  in  line  with  the  target  set  by  the  Development  Budget  Coordination 
Committee (DBCC).22

slower revenue growth [Press release].

<!-- PAGE 41 -->

NG  revenues  totaled  ₱4.45 trillion  in  2025,  up  marginally  by  0.8 percent  from 
₱4.42 trillion in 2024 but 1.5 percent below the full-year program. Revenue growth 
decelerated  sharply  from  15.6 percent  in  the  previous  year.  This  was  partly  
due  to  changes  in  audit  operations  and  the  suspension  of  payments  for 
infrastructure-related contracts, which dampened withholding tax collections.

Revenue  collection  was  further  weighed  down  by  a  sharp  decline  in  non-tax 
revenues, which fell from ₱618.3 billion to ₱376.3 billion, reﬂecting the absence of 
one-off windfall receipts. As a result, revenue effort softened to 15.9 percent of GDP, 
in line with the full-year program but below the 16.7 percent outturn in 2024.23

NG  disbursements  reached  ₱6.03 trillion  in  2025,  rising  by  1.8 percent  from  
₱5.93  trillion  a  year  ago,  but  falling  0.9  percent  below  the  full-year  program. 
The increase  in  spending  was  driven  by  higher  allocations  to  local  government 
units  (LGUs)  through  the  National  Tax  Allotment,  expanded  personnel  services 
expenditures due to the implementation of the second tranche of compensation 
adjustments  for  civil  servants,  and  increased  maintenance  and  other  operating 
expenditures,  largely  for  education  programs.  Nonetheless,  expenditure  growth 
softened  amid  governance  concerns  and  adverse  weather  conditions  that 
hampered  infrastructure  project  implementation.  As  a  result,  expenditure  effort 
declined to 21.5 percent in 2025, slightly above the 21.4 percent full-year program 
but below the 22.4 percent outturn in 2024.24

Similar  to  the  previous  year,  the  NG  ﬁnanced  the  budget  deﬁcit  mainly  through 
domestic sources, with an 80:20 domestic-to-external financing mix. As of end-2025, 
NG outstanding debt stood at 63.2 percent of GDP, exceeding both the 60.7 percent 
outturn in 2024 and the 61.3 percent DBCC program.25

Amid heightened uncertainty, the NG remains committed to a growth-enhancing 
ﬁscal consolidation strategy. This approach is anchored on tighter ﬁscal discipline 
while continuing to support structural reforms and high-impact projects.

Financial market conditions

The  global  economy  remained  resilient  in  2025  despite  heightened  policy 
uncertainty, tariff-related volatility, and persistent fiscal pressures. Market sentiment 
shifted  between  optimism  and  caution,  as  changing  trade  policies,  geopolitical 
developments, rapid advances in AI, and evolving central bank guidance shaped 
the outlook.

The  return  of  US  President  Donald  Trump  ushered  in  a  new  phase  of  US 
protectionism, with successive tariff rounds affecting Canada, Mexico, China, and 
the European Union (EU). Market pressures eased following a 90-day tariff pause 
between  the  US  and  China,  along  with  a  series  of  mid-year  trade  agreements, 
culminating in a US–China truce that helped stabilize supply chains.

Although  tariff  measures  initially  raised  concerns  about  global  inﬂation  and 
growth, subsequent developments—mainly in the US—helped temper inﬂationary 
pressures.  These  included  the  tariff  pause,  partial  cost  pass-through  as  ﬁrms 
absorbed margins, supply chain adjustments, and softening demand. As a result, 
US core inﬂation eased during the year, reducing stagﬂation risks, although growth 
concerns persisted.

On the ﬁscal side, mounting strains dampened investor sentiment. The US faced  
a  sovereign  downgrade  (Moody’s:  Aaa  to  Aa1  on  16  May  2025),  a  US$4.0 trillion 
spending  bill,  and  the  longest  government  shutdown,  underscoring  rising  debt 
vulnerabilities. Likewise, debt levels in Europe remained elevated, especially in the 
United Kingdom (UK) and France.

US monetary policy expectations shifted throughout the year before settling into a 
gradual  easing  cycle.  After  holding  rates  steady  for  most  of  the  year,  the  Federal 
Reserve (Fed) delivered three 25-basis point (bp) cuts in September, October, and 
December,  and  ended  quantitative  tightening.  Meanwhile,  the  Bank  of  England 
and the European Central Bank each reduced rates four times. In contrast, the Bank 
of Japan raised its policy rate to a three-decade high of 0.75 percent.

Geopolitical  tensions,  including  renewed  conﬂict  in  the  Middle  East,  and  rapid 
AI-driven  market  shifts—such  as  concerns  over  a  potential  valuation  bubble  
amid  soaring  valuations,  with  Nvidia’s  market  cap  surpassing  US$4.0 trillion—
added to global uncertainty, reinforcing the need for prudent policy management 
across economies.

14 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 43 -->

Maintenance of peso stability

In line with its commitment to preserve the stability and convertibility of the peso, 
the BSP continued to closely monitor the US$/₱ market. The Bank maintained its 
presence to manage exchange rate volatility and ensure orderly market conditions.

The  peso’s  volatility  declined  from  the  previous  year’s  and  remained  within  the 
mid-range  of  regional  currencies  monitored.26  In  terms  of  market  participation,  
the BSP was a net seller for the months of January to April, June, and October to 
December. Meanwhile, it was a net buyer for the months of May and July. The BSP 
had a neutral position in August and September.

The peso depreciated by 1.6 percent, moving from ₱57.845/US$1 on 27 December 2024 
to  ₱58.790/US$1  on  29  December  2025  (range: ₱55.143–₱59.260).  The  currency 
initially appreciated, reaching a high of ₱55.143/US$1, supported by broad US dollar 
weakness. This reﬂected uncertainty over US trade policy, expectations of Fed rate 
cuts, and gains in major currencies amid region-speciﬁc policy developments.

However,  the  peso  later  reversed  its  gains  and  depreciated.  This  was  driven  by 
heightened geopolitical tensions, the BSP’s more accommodative monetary policy 
stance, and weaker investor sentiment due to corruption concerns.

Nonetheless,  the  BSP  maintained  prudent  and  calibrated  market  participation, 
consistent  with  its  mandate  to  promote  price  stability  and  support  orderly  
market conditions.

Credit rating assessment

its

The  Philippines  maintained 
investment-grade  credit  ratings  from  all 
international debt watchers. Notably, S&P Global Ratings afﬁrmed its “BBB+” rating 
with  a  positive  outlook.  Moody’s    (“Baa2”),  Fitch  Ratings  (“BBB”),  Rating  and 
Investment Information, Inc. (R&I) (“A-”), and Japan Credit Rating Agency (“A-”) also 
maintained  their  ratings  with  stable  outlooks,  despite  domestic  and  global 
challenges.  These  affirmations  reflect  the  Philippines’  above-average  economic 
growth and strong external position. Credit rating agencies also underscored the 
country’s  stable  banking  system  and  effective  policymaking,  which  support 
resilient economic performance and sustained public investment.

Investor engagements

In  2025,  the  BSP  intensiﬁed  efforts  to  position  the  Philippines  as  a  competitive 
destination  for  global  investment.  Through  targeted  outreach,  engagement  
with  credit  rating  agencies,  and  collaboration  with  key  stakeholders,  the  BSP 
communicated  the  country’s  economic  developments,  reform  agenda,  and 
socioeconomic  priorities  to  both  domestic  and 
international  audiences. 
These initiatives  enhanced  transparency,  strengthened  the  country’s  economic 
narrative,  reinforced  investor  conﬁdence,  and  supported  favorable  credit  rating 
assessments.

26  Regional  currencies  monitored  include  the  Indonesian  rupiah,  Indian  rupee,  Korean  won,  Malaysian  ringgit, 
Taiwan dollar, and Thai baht.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 15

---

<!-- PAGE 44 -->

A  key  platform  was  the  series  of  Philippine  Economic  Briefings  (PEBs)  and 
economic  dialogues  held  locally  and  abroad.  In  2025,  the  BSP  organized  three 
PEBs in Cebu, Clark, and Cagayan de Oro. The first Philippine Economic Dialogue 
in  Milan,  Italy  convened  around  100  European  business  leaders,  bankers,  and 
investors,  while  the  PEB  in  Osaka,  Japan  drew  over  280  Japanese  investors 
and stakeholders.

These  events  enabled  Philippine  economic  officials  to  present  updates  on 
economic  performance  and  policy  priorities  while  engaging  directly  with  global 
stakeholders.  Discussions  highlighted  the  country’s  strong  macroeconomic 
fundamentals,  expanding  domestic  market,  young  and  skilled  workforce,  and 
ongoing reforms and infrastructure initiatives that support a conducive business 
environment and sustained investment.

Complementing these efforts, the BSP facilitated 93 investor meetings: 65 in Manila 
and 28 on the sidelines of the IMF–WB meetings, international PEBs, and non-deal 
roadshows.  These  engagements  provided  a  platform  for  Philippine  economic 
managers to share insights on macroeconomic trends, policy directions, and the 
medium-term  outlook  while  allowing  investors  to  discuss  risks  and  investment 
prospects.

The BSP also coordinated 85 meetings with credit rating agencies, focusing on the 
country’s  economic  outlook;  fiscal  consolidation  plans;  progress  on  key  reforms, 
such  as  the  Corporate  Recovery  and  Tax  Incentives  for  Enterprises  to  Maximize 
Opportunities  for  Reinvigorating  the  Economy  (CREATE  MORE)  Act;  budget 
priorities; and the overall business environment.

With stronger engagement with the global investment community, the Philippines 
ranked  among  the  top  performers  in  the  2025  Institute  of  International  Finance 
Investor Relations and Debt Transparency Survey.27 The country placed second out 
of 54 emerging and frontier markets, with a near-perfect score of  49.3 out of 50. 
The Philippines  also  ranked  third  in  debt  transparency  and  received  a  perfect 
score for  the  dissemination  of  environmental,  social,  and  governance  (ESG)  data 
and policies.

27 The survey evaluates countries based on their investor relations practices, debt transparency, and dissemination

<!-- PAGE 45 -->

External sector

Balance of payments

The Philippines recorded a full-year balance-of-payments (BOP) deﬁcit equivalent 
to 1.2 percent of GDP in 2025, reversing the surplus recorded in 2024. Softer ﬁnancial 
account  inﬂows  more  than  offset  the  improvement  in  the  current  account. 
The current account deficit narrowed from 4.0 percent of GDP in 2024 to 3.3 percent 
in 2025, driven largely by a smaller trade-in-goods deﬁcit.

The  external  trade  position  improved  due  to  stronger  global  demand  for 
technology-related goods. Firms frontloaded shipments ahead of the announced  
US  reciprocal  tariff  revisions  in  mid-2025,  reﬂecting  an  intertemporal  shift  in  
trade activity. Meanwhile, imports expanded amid easing supply chain constraints, 
resilient  industrial  activity  in  AEs,  and  sustained  investment  in  digitalization  
and electriﬁcation.

Goods  exports  rose  by  15.2 percent  from  US$55.1 billion  in  2024  to  US$63.4 billion 
in 2025.  Growth  was  led  by  electronic  products,  reﬂecting  robust global  demand 
for  semiconductors,  electronic  data  processing  equipment,  and  components  
used  in  AI  applications  and  electric  vehicles.  Exports  of  other manufactured  and 
mineral products also strengthened, including higher shipments of copper anodes,  
nickel  ores,  and  copper  mattes,  underpinned  by  ﬁrm  global  industrial  demand  
and favorable metal prices. Export growth was mainly driven by higher shipments 
to Hong Kong, the US, and Japan.

Goods  imports  expanded  by  5.0  percent  from  US$123.9  billion  in  2024  to 
US$130.1 billion  in  2025,  reﬂecting  resilient  domestic  demand.  Higher  imports  of 
telecommunications  equipment  and  electrical  machinery  mirrored  continued 
investments  in  digital  infrastructure.  Increased  imports  of  passenger  cars  and 
motorcycles, 
including  electric  mopeds,  pointed  to  sustained  demand  for  
transport  solutions.  Imports  of  animal  and  vegetable  oils  and  fats  also  rose.  
Import  growth  was  largely  driven  by  China,  South  Korea,  and  Taiwan,  consistent 
with deepening regional production linkages.

Net receipts from trade in services fell by 4.5 percent. This was weighed down by 
higher outbound travel and increased spending on computer and financial services. 
Nonetheless, services exports continued to beneﬁt from BPO revenues, which grew 
by 4.8 percent despite weaker travel receipts. Net primary income receipts declined 
by  6.1 percent  due  to  higher  dividend  and  interest  payments  to  non-residents. 
In contrast,  net  secondary  income  receipts  rose  by  3.1 percent,  driven  by  record 
remittances from overseas Filipino workers (OFWs).

Financial  account 
inﬂows  moderated  amid  heightened  global  uncertainty. 
Net inﬂows  declined  from  US$19.1  billion  in  2024  to  US$10.9  billion  in  2025. 
This reﬂected  a  shift  in  portfolio  investment  to  net  outﬂows  and  reduced  inﬂows  
in  both  direct  and  other  investments.  Net  direct  investment  inﬂows  declined  by 
7.6 percent from US$6.7 billion to US$6.2 billion, as net foreign direct investments 
fell  by  17.1 percent.  This  was  partly  offset  by  a  31.4-percent  rise  in  net  equity  
capital  placements—mainly  from  Japan,  the  US,  and  Singapore—directed  toward 
manufacturing, trade, and ﬁnancial services.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 17

---

<!-- PAGE 46 -->

The  portfolio  investment  account  posted  net  outﬂows  of  US$1.3  billion,  reversing 
net  inﬂows  of  US$3.5  billion  in  2024.  This  shift  was  driven  by  a  sharp  increase  in 
resident investments in foreign debt securities, which rose from US$430 million to 
US$4.6 billion, and lower non-resident holdings of government long-term bonds. 
Other  investment  inﬂows  declined  by  29.7  percent  from  US$8.6  billion  to 
US$6.0 billion,  reﬂecting  banks’  loan  repayments  and  reduced  government 
borrowings.  Overall,  sustained  direct  and  other  investment  inﬂows,  alongside 
outward  portfolio  diversiﬁcation,  contributed  to  a  more  balanced  external 
ﬁnancing proﬁle and reinforced external resilience.

Meanwhile, the narrower current account deﬁcit corresponds to a smaller negative 
saving–investment  gap,  consistent  with  the  standard  macroeconomic  identity 
linking external and domestic resource balances. From this perspective, the smaller 
gap reﬂects moderation in both gross saving and gross investment, shaping the 
observed  external  adjustment.  The  slowdown  appears  to  have  followed  stronger 
activity  in  the  ﬁrst  half  of  the  year,  when  consumption  and  investment  were 
relatively ﬁrmer, before easing in the second half amid weaker conﬁdence linked to 
the ﬂood control controversy.

Gross  saving  likely  softened  as  household  consumption  remained  supported  by 
lower inﬂation, steady remittance inﬂows, and rising wages. At the same time, gross 
investment  decelerated  amid  cautious  business  sentiment,  softer  external 
demand,  and  more  conservative  inventory  and  capital  spending  decisions. 
Indicators such as softening business conﬁdence, uneven PMI trends, and slower 
growth in goods imports are consistent with this moderation in private sector activity.

Despite the narrowing of the current account deficit, its persistence raises questions 
about the long-run sustainability of the Philippines’ external position. An empirical 
study by the BSP using updated data for Q1 2005–Q4 2025 found that the country’s 
current  account  exhibits  weak  or  conditional  sustainability,28  indicating  that 
external payment obligations are not fully matched by proportional export growth. 
This  result  reﬂects  structural  features  of  the  Philippine  external  sector,  where 
adjustment  relies  not  only  on  goods  exports  but  also  on  remittances,  BPO  and 
other services receipts, and stable capital inﬂows. In addition, recent deﬁcits have 
been  largely  investment-driven,  associated  with  infrastructure  development  and 
capital  formation  rather  than  consumption,  with  medium-term  repayment 
capacity supported by higher potential growth.

Taken together, these conditions suggest that external adjustment is shaped not 
only by trade performance but also by the resilience of services and income ﬂows, 
the  quality  of 
investment,  and  the  broader  macroeconomic  environment 
supporting external ﬁnancing and investor conﬁdence.

28 Esguerra, A. (2024). Assessing current account sustainability and its determinants through a panel regression 
analysis: The case of the Philippines and select ASEAN economies [Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas Discussion Paper 
Series No. 2024-01]. https://www.bsp.gov.ph/Sites/researchsite/Publications/BSP-Discussion-Papers/DP202401.pdf

<!-- PAGE 47 -->

Gross international reserves

Gross international reserves (GIR) reached US$110.8 billion as of end-December 2025, 
up from US$106.3 billion in 2024. The reserve level was sufficient to cover 7.3 months’ 
worth  of  imports  of  goods  and  payments  for  services  and  primary income.  
It was also equivalent to 4.1 times the country’s short-term external debt based on 
residual maturity. The y-o-y increase in reserves was mainly driven by higher market 
valuations of the BSP’s gold holdings.

Foreign investments accounted for 78.4 percent of total reserves, followed by gold 
at 16.8 percent. The remaining 4.8 percent consisted of holdings of Special Drawing 
Rights, foreign exchange (FX), and the Reserve Position in the IMF.

Net international reserves, or the difference between the GIR and the BSP’s total 
short-term  liabilities,  amounted  to  US$110.8  billion  as  of  end-December  2025. 
This marked an increase of US$4.6 billion from US$106.2 billion in the previous year.

External debt

The  Philippines’  external  debt  rose  by  7.3 percent  y-o-y  to  US$147.7  billion  as  of 
end-December  2025.  The  increase  was  driven  by  development-related  public 
sector  borrowings  and  private  sector  ﬁnancing  to  support  liquidity  needs.29 
Despite this,  the  external  debt  position  remained  manageable  and  sustainable, 
supported  by 
indicators  and  ample  FX  buffers.  
These conditions underscore the economy’s continued capacity to meet external 
obligations over the medium to long term.

improved  debt  servicing

Figure 7 
External Debt and External Debt-to-Gross Domestic Product 
levels in billion dollars; ratio in percent

Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas

29 Development-related public sector borrowings refer to loans and other debt instruments contracted by the national 
government and public sector entities to finance projects and programs that support long-term economic and social

2025 BSP Annual Report | 19

---

<!-- PAGE 48 -->

External liquidity buffers remained robust. The GIR was sufﬁcient to cover 4.1 times 
the  country’s  short-term  external  debt  based  on  the  remaining  maturity  basis.30 
The debt service ratio—which relates principal and interest payments to exports of 
goods and receipts from services and primary income—improved from 11.5 percent 
in  2024  to  8.3 percent  in  2025,  reﬂecting  stronger  FX  earnings  and  lower  debt 
servicing  requirements.  The  external  debt-to-GDP  ratio  stood  at  30.3 percent, 
comparing favorably with selected peer economies.

Key drivers

The  increase  in  external  debt  was  mainly  driven  by  NG  borrowings,  including 
US$3.3 billion in global bond issuances and US$6.1 billion in loans from multilateral 
and bilateral creditors. These funds were directed toward infrastructure development, 
social  protection,  and  support  for  agriculture  and  industry,  consistent  with  
the government’s medium-term development priorities and long-run productivity 
objectives.

The  private  sector,  particularly  banks,  also  contributed  to  higher  external  debt,  
with  institutions  securing  US$3.7  billion  in  offshore  ﬁnancing  to  meet  liquidity 
requirements.  These  borrowings  supported  balance  sheet  management  and 
ﬁnancial intermediation amid changing domestic and global ﬁnancial conditions.

Valuation  effects  and  capital  market  dynamics  further  inﬂuenced  external  debt 
levels.  Upward  FX  revaluation  added  US$1.3  billion  to  the  debt  stock,  reﬂecting  
the  depreciation  of  the  US  dollar  against  major  currencies  in  2025.  In  addition,  
net  non-resident  acquisitions  of  Philippine  debt  securities  amounting  to 
US$1.2 billion  contributed  to  the  increase,  indicating  generally  favorable  foreign 
investor sentiment for most of the year.

Policy implications

The  2025  external  debt  outturn  highlights  the  role  of  external  ﬁnancing  as  a 
complement  to  domestic  resources 
in  advancing  development  priorities. 
Borrowing  should  remain  anchored  on  high-impact  investments  that  enhance 
productive capacity while staying consistent with medium-term ﬁscal and external 
sustainability.

Safeguarding  FX-earning  capacity  is  key  to  limiting  external  vulnerabilities. 
Strong exports, services receipts, remittances, and adequate reserves help ensure 
that higher borrowing does not translate into heightened risk.

Prudent  external  debt  management,  therefore,  remains  essential.  Policy  should 
continue  to  support  a  sound  maturity  proﬁle,  manage  currency  risks  effectively, 
and maintain sufficient buffers against volatile global financial conditions, alongside 
close monitoring of capital ﬂows and external ﬁnancing terms.

30 Short-term accounts under the remaining maturity concept comprise loans with original maturities of 1 year or 
less, plus amortizations on medium- and long-term accounts falling due within the next 12 months.

Pursuing price stability through timely initiatives

Monetary stability

The BSP reduces the target reverse repurchase rate.

In  2025,  the  BSP  reduced  the  target  reverse  repurchase  (RRP)  rate—the  key  policy 
interest rate—by a cumulative 125 bps to 4.50 percent. The interest rates on the overnight 
deposit  and  lending  facilities  were  also  reduced  to  4.00 percent  and  5.00 percent, 
respectively.

Domestic  demand  remained  ﬁrm  in  the  ﬁrst  half  of  the  year.  However,  economic 
growth  subsequently  weakened  amid  lingering  uncertainty  over  global  trade  policy. 
Governance concerns also weighed on market sentiment and tempered investment and 
consumption.  With  a  benign 
inﬂation 
expectations, the BSP saw scope for monetary policy to reinforce market confidence and 
support domestic demand.

inﬂation  outlook  and  well-anchored

At  its  policy  meeting  in  December 2025,  the  Monetary  Board  (MB)  noted  that  the 
effects  of  prior  monetary  policy  easing  continued  to  work  through  the  economy. 
Domestic  growth  is  expected  to  rebound  gradually  over  the  policy  horizon,  partly 
reﬂecting these lagged effects.

Looking  ahead,  the  BSP  will  ensure  that  overall  monetary  policy  settings  remain 
consistent with maintaining price stability conducive to sustainable economic growth. 
Future policy decisions will remain data-dependent.

Monetary operations

Management of domestic liquidity

The BSP continues to implement monetary policy through its key instruments. These 
include the overnight RRP facility, the term deposit facility (TDF), and the BSP securities 
facility (BSP-SF), as well as its two standing facilities: the overnight deposit facility (ODF) 
and the overnight lending facility (OLF).

To  align  monetary  operations  with  global  practices,  the  BSP  adopted  Global  Master 
Repurchase  Agreement  (GMRA)-based  RRP  and  OLF  transactions  and  automated  
the  OLF.  These  enhancements  improved  the  standardization  and  transparency  of 
monetary operations and reduced operational risks associated with manual processes.

The  BSP  also  shifted  to  single-tenor  term  facilities  as  part  of  a  broader  strategy  to 
increase  reliance  on  overnight  instruments.  The  rationalization  of  tenors  aims  to 
encourage interbank trading and promote more market-driven liquidity allocation. The 
shift  also  supports  the  development  of  a  well-functioning  term  structure  that  better 
aligns with market fundamentals.

As  of  29 December 2025,  outstanding  placements  in  the  central  bank’s  monetary 
instruments—particularly  the  RRP  facility,  the  ODF,  the  TDF,  and  the  BSP-SF—
amounted to ₱1.5 trillion. This was a 16.5-percent y-o-y decline (₱280.0 billion) from the 
₱1.7 trillion outstanding placements as of 27 December 2024.

24 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 53 -->

The  composition  of  placements  also  shifted  toward  overnight  instruments  (RRP and 
ODF), consistent with the reduction of the tenors offered. The share of term facilities 
(TDF  and  BSP-SF)  declined  from  53.0 percent  in  2024  to  28.0 percent  in  2025,  while 
overnight  facilities  (RRP  and  ODF)  expanded  from  ₱818.8  billion  (47.0  percent)  to 
₱1.1 trillion (72.0 percent) over the same period.

Meanwhile, total availments under the OLF reached ₱5.2 trillion, 5.6 times higher than 
the  ₱932.4 billion  recorded  in  the  previous  year.  Banks  borrowed  from  the  OLF  to 
support loan releases, client withdrawals, deposit outﬂows, government securities (GS) 
purchases, business continuity plan testing, and compliance with reserve requirements.

In  the  money  markets,  banks  access  funding  through  three  main  segments: 
the interbank  call  loan  (IBCL)  market,  the  GS  repurchase  (repo)  market,  and  the  FX 
swaps market. The sum of repos and IBCL volumes increased by 2.30 times y-o-y, from 
₱6.9 trillion in 2024 to ₱15.8 trillion in 2025. Average daily IBCL borrowings rose by 2.33 
times  from  ₱13.0  billion  to  ₱30.4  billion,  reﬂecting  continued  recovery  in  market 
activity. Meanwhile, total repo volume increased by 111 percent to ₱1.02 trillion, as more 
banks participated and increasingly viewed repos as a cost-effective funding alternative.

Loans and credit

The BSP provides efﬁcient and responsive credit operations in its role as lender of last 
resort (LOLR). The discount window facility (DWF) remained available to banks facing 
temporary liquidity needs throughout the year.

As of 31 December 2025, 38 banks maintained active credit lines with the BSP, totaling 
₱348.4 billion. These included 22 rural and cooperative banks (RCBs), 10 universal and 
commercial banks (U/KBs), and 6 thrift banks (TBs). Total DWF loan availments reached 
₱60.1 million in 2025.

Principal  collections  rose  signiﬁcantly  from  ₱300.9 million  in  2024  to  ₱1.8 billion  
in 2025. The increase largely reﬂected the settlement of ﬁnancial assistance loans by 
the  Philippine  Deposit  Insurance  Corporation  (PDIC)  and  the  recovery  of  past-due 
accounts,31 amounting to ₱1.5 billion. The remaining ₱316.9 million came from current 
accounts, including DWF availments and notes receivable–certiﬁcate of indebtedness.

long-outstanding  
in  past-due 
The  BSP  wrote  off  ₱2.8 billion 
legacy issues. Together with principal collections, these actions reduced long-past-due 
accounts  by  95.2 percent—from  ₱4.5 billion  as  of  end-2024  to  ₱219.3 million  as  of 
end-2025. As a result, total outstanding loans and receivables declined to ₱92.4 billion, 
down from ₱96.9 billion as of 31 December 2024.

loans,  resolving

These loan portfolio clean-up efforts allowed the BSP to refocus its credit operations 
from resolving legacy exposures to strengthening its core mandate as LOLR. The BSP 
also  engaged  with  peer  central  banks  to  adopt  international  best  practices  in  LOLR 
frameworks and further enhance its credit operations.

International operations

The  BSP  helps  ensure  that  the  country’s  external  debt  remains  sustainable.  In  2025,  
the  MB  approved  US$12.7 billion  in  public  sector  medium-  to  long-term  foreign 
borrowings.  Project  loans  accounted  for  US$5.9 billion  (46.3 percent),  supporting 
infrastructure,  transportation,  health  services,  and  civil  service  modernization.  Bond 
issuance totaling US$3.4 billion (27.0 percent) ﬁnanced the NG’s general requirements 
for  2025.  Program  loans  totaling  US$3.4 billion  (26.7 percent)  supported  economic 
development, climate resilience, ﬁnance, and social protection initiatives.

The BSP also facilitates private sector foreign borrowings, including bonds, notes, and 
other debt instruments. In 2025, the central bank registered 310 private sector foreign 
loans amounting to US$7.6 billion and issued 148 amendments to their financial terms.32 
These  borrowings  were  used  for  general  corporate  purposes,  relending,  capital 
expenditures,  and  working  capital.  The  BSP  also  registered  guarantees  totaling 
US$366.2 million.

Direct foreign investments

investments.  
The  BSP  continues  to  support  policies  that  encourage  foreign 
For  the  full  year  2025,  foreign  investments  registered  with  the  BSP  aggregated 
US$1,916.3 million. Of this total, US$1,896.0 million (98.9 percent) was funded mainly by 
cash through inward or constructive remittances. The remaining was funded by cash 
and stock dividends (US$16.3 million or 0.9 percent) and reinvestments (US$3.8 million 
or less than 1.0 percent).

These  funds  mainly  came  from  the  Netherlands  (US$938.0  million),  Singapore 
(US$528.3 million),  and  Japan  (US$122.5 million).  Financial  and  insurance  activities 
received the largest share (US$1.3 billion), followed by wholesale and retail trade; repair of 
motor vehicles and motorcycles (US$122.7 million) and manufacturing (US$116.2 million).

Foreign investments through authorized agent banks

Foreign investments registered with the BSP through authorized agent banks (AABs) 
from  January  to  December  2025  totaled  US$27.9 billion,  a  55.4-percent  increase 
(US$9.9 billion)  from  US$17.9 billion  in  2024.  These  investments  were  largely  placed  in 
peso-denominated GS (69.6 percent).33 The remainder was invested in Philippine Stock 
Exchange-listed  securities  (30.4 percent)—mostly  in  banks;  transportation  services; 
holding  ﬁrms;  property;  and  electricity,  energy,  power,  and  water—and  other 
investments (less than one percent).

The  UK,  Singapore,  US,  Luxembourg,  and  Hong Kong  were  the  top  ﬁve  investor 
countries during the year, with a combined share of 90.0 percent.

<!-- PAGE 55 -->

Overall,  transactions  from  01 January  to  31 December 2025  yielded  net  inﬂows  of 
US$1.6 billion.  This  was  lower  by  US$479.5 million  (22.8 percent)  compared  with  the 
US$2.1 billion net inﬂows recorded for the same period in 2024.

Market developments influenced investment flows, including policy rate adjustments by 
the US Fed and the BSP, favorable growth assessments by credit rating agencies, and 
the relative attractiveness of peso GS. These factors were partly offset by global trade 
policy uncertainty and domestic governance concerns.

Foreign exchange policy initiatives

Consistent with its commitment to a sound and inclusive policy environment, the BSP 
pursues  policy  initiatives  and  liberalization  measures  to  keep  the  FX  regulatory 
framework responsive to a dynamic and expanding economy.

In  April 2025,  the  BSP  introduced  reforms  to  further  develop  the  FX  derivatives 
market.34  These  included  expanding  eligible  instruments  and  hedging  transactions, 
clarifying  hedging  rules  for  AABs,  permitting  access  to  offshore  non-deliverable 
forward markets for legitimate purposes, and lifting the co-terminus requirement for 
deliverable  forward  contracts.  The  BSP  also  clariﬁed,  through  a  memorandum,  the 
regulatory  treatment  of  AAB-afﬁliated  FX  corporations  under  the  Manual  of 
Regulations  for  Non-Bank  Financial  Institutions,  while  maintaining  FX  transaction 
rules under the FX Manual.35

To support digitalization, the BSP updated guidelines on the electronic submission of 
applications,  requests,  and  other  requirements.36,37  The  central  bank  also  issued  an 
advisory on the implementation of the Foreign Loans and Investments Management 
System–Investment  Module,  which  outlines  the  procedures  for  the  electronic 
submission of applications for registration of inward investments under Section 36.1 of 
the FX Manual, including related requests and requirements.38

International reserves management

The  BSP  manages  the  GIR  with  prudence  and  foresight.  In  2025,  the  central  bank 
implemented  strategic  reforms  and  initiatives  designed  to  deliver  consistent  returns, 
reinforce  risk  management,  and  improve  operational  efﬁciency.  These  included 
changes  to  the  reserve  management  framework,  resulting  in  streamlined  reserve 
tranches  and  a  more  risk-return-focused  strategic  asset  allocation  (SAA)  that 
incorporates  tail-risk  measures.  Enhanced  internal  capabilities  and  technology  also 
allowed the BSP to manage some outsourced portfolios in-house, reducing costs and 
increasing control over reserve deployment.

<!-- PAGE 56 -->

In  its  operations,  the  BSP  applied  investment  strategies  to  enhance  returns  and 
diversify risks, while adhering to the objectives and risk limits set and approved by the 
MB.  As  part  of  the  periodic  SAA  review,  international  reserve  tranches  and  portfolios 
were  rebalanced  to  ensure  their  adequacy  in  supporting  domestic  operations, 
reinforcing capital preservation, and enhancing risk-adjusted returns.

The  BSP’s  internally-managed  portfolio  performed  well  against  the  benchmark. 
Positive  duration  and  favorable  curve  positioning,  amid  declining  yields  and  a 
steepening yield curve, supported performance.

The  BSP  also  engaged  the  services  of  external  fund  managers.  Their  performance 
exceeded  benchmarks,  driven  by  effective  interest  rate  strategies,  timely  duration 
positioning, and selective exposures across global and regional bond markets.

Moreover, the BSP managed its gold reserves within approved investment parameters, 
maintaining a strategic level of exposure to gold assets within the GIR.

International relations and economic cooperation

The  BSP  continues  to  strengthen  regional  and  global  monetary  and  ﬁnancial 
cooperation. In 2025, the Bank contributed to reinforcing the global ﬁnancial safety net 
by expanding access to liquidity buffers and BOP support mechanisms.

Key  milestones  included  the  BSP’s  leadership  in  reestablishing  the  Association  
of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) Swap Arrangement, enhancements to the Chiang 
Mai Initiative Multilateralization through the introduction of a Rapid Financing Facility, 
the renewal of the bilateral swap arrangement with the Bank of Japan, and support for 
an increase in the Philippines’ IMF quota.

In  preparation  for  the  Philippines  chairing  ASEAN  in  2026,  the  BSP  intensiﬁed 
coordination with ASEAN members, dialogue partners, and domestic agencies. These 
initiatives  helped  shape  a  forward-looking  agenda  on  cross-border  payments,  capital 
market development, local currency use, ﬁnancial health, and sustainable ﬁnance. As 
Co-Chair of the ASEAN Working Committee on Capital Account Liberalization,39 the BSP 
advanced  initiatives  that  promote  orderly  liberalization  and  effective  capital  ﬂow 
management.

The BSP also maintained close engagement with international institutions and central 
banks,  contributing  to  policy  dialogues,  technical  cooperation,  and  multilateral 
workstreams on macroeconomic developments, ﬁnancial stability, digital innovation, 
and payments.

In support of the country’s trade and investment agenda, the BSP provided technical 
inputs  to  bilateral  and  multilateral  negotiations  on  ﬁnancial  services,  capital  and 
currency movements, and prudential measures, ensuring alignment with its mandates.

Overall,  these  initiatives  strengthened  regional  financial  cooperation  and  enhanced 
institutional readiness for the Philippines chairing ASEAN in 2026.
