Financial health and inclusion

Financial inclusion in the Philippines made further gains in 2025, driven by growing 
shared  ﬁnancial  access  within  households.  Household-level  account  ownership 
increased  from  74.0 percent  in  2024  to  85.0 percent  in  2025,62  reﬂecting  broader 
participation in the formal ﬁnancial system.

Beyond  expanding  access,  the  BSP  emphasized  the  effective  use  of  ﬁnancial 
services  as  a  measure  of  meaningful  inclusion.  True  progress  lies  in  improving 
ﬁnancial  health,  or  the  ability  of  Filipinos  to  meet  daily  expenses,  withstand 
ﬁnancial shocks, plan for the future, and manage their ﬁnances with conﬁdence. 
Guided  by  the  2022–2028  National  Strategy  for  Financial  Inclusion  (NSFI),  BSP 
initiatives  during  the  year  focused  on  two  priorities:  (a) onboarding  excluded 
Filipinos into the formal ﬁnancial system and (b) promoting ﬁnancial health as the 
next frontier of ﬁnancial inclusion.

Expanding access for the unbanked

The  BSP  continued  promoting  basic  deposit  accounts  (BDAs)  as  an  entry-level 
product  for  underserved  sectors.  By  2025,  BDA  accounts  reached  27.6 million, 
up from 25.8 million in 2024, with 170 banks offering BDAs.

Targeted  engagement  with  priority  sectors  also  progressed.  For  persons  with 
disabilities (PWDs), the BSP, in partnership with the National Council for Disability 
Affairs, conducted regional focus group discussions to better understand barriers 
to  ﬁnancial  access.  Key  recommendations  included  institutionalizing  sensitivity 
training  for  bank  personnel,  improving  accessibility  of  digital  ﬁnancial  services, 
strengthening  recognition  of  PWD  identiﬁcation  cards  for  ﬁnancial  transactions, 
and enhancing targeted financial education. Findings were shared with stakeholders 
to inform policy and industry practices in November 2025.

To  advance  youth  inclusion,  the  BSP  and  the  Financial  Inclusion  Steering 
Committee  (FISC)  launched  the  Youth  Financial  Inclusion  (YFI)  initiative  in 
in  April  2025,  which 
January 2025.  This was  followed  by  the YFI  Congress 
introduced  the  ﬁrst  cohort  of  YFI  ambassadors—student  leaders  tasked  with 
promoting  ﬁnancial 
in  their  communities—and  recognized  top 
hackathon proposals offering innovative solutions. Ambassadors from Luzon and 
Visayas  later  implemented  action  plans  with  BSP  support,  helping  cultivate  the 
next generation of ﬁnancial inclusion advocates.

Diversifying pathways to credit

MSMEs  are  central  to  economic  growth  yet  continue  to  face  credit  gaps. 
In response, the BSP reinforced both traditional and innovative ﬁnancing solutions 
to expand MSME access to ﬁnance.

Microﬁnance  remained  a  key  channel 
for  microentrepreneurs.  By  2025, 
microﬁnance  loans  reached  ₱36.9 billion,  beneﬁting  2.0 million  borrowers  across 
126  participating  banks.  Operations  covered  92.2 percent  of  LGUs,  including 
62.5 percent of unbanked areas, extending last-mile access to formal ﬁnance.63

To broaden collateral options, the BSP is laying the groundwork for movable asset 
ﬁnancing (MAF), which allows MSMEs to use inventory, equipment, and receivables 
as  loan  security.  In  March  2025,  the  Bank  signed  an  MOU  with  the  International 
Finance  Corporation  for  the  Philippine  Movables  Finance  Market  Development 
Project  2025–2027.  Joint  roundtables  on  supply  chain  ﬁnance  and  the  Personal 
Property Security Registry supported this effort. The approval of the MAF Roadmap, 
under the NSFI, in 2025 set the strategic direction through 2028.

The  Credit  Surety  Fund  (CSF)  continued  to  enhance  MSME  creditworthiness  by 
providing up to 80.0 percent surety cover for bank loans. As of 31 December 2025, 
50 CSF cooperatives were registered, including ﬁve new ones, with ₱9.2 billion in 
released  loans.64  Capacity-building  programs,  LGU  orientations,  and  the  CSF 
Spotlight  Forum  Series  supported  nationwide  promotion  and  operational 
strengthening.

The BSP also supports the use of credit assessment tools for risk-based lending to 
businesses.  Among  these  is  the  Credit  Risk  Database  (CRD),  which  was  jointly 
developed by the BSP and the Japan International Cooperation Agency. The CRD 
addresses the funding challenges faced by small and medium enterprises, often 
referred  to  as  the  “missing  middle.”  In  2025,  Phase  II  of  the  CRD  marked  a 
signiﬁcant  milestone  with  the  launch  of  its  web-based  scoring  system  and  the 
rollout of nationwide information campaigns.

To  simplify  loan  processes,  the  BSP  promoted  the  Standard  Business  Loan 
Application Form through issuing FAQs and conducting roadshows. The Bank also 
partnered with the Department of Trade and Industry (DTI) and other agencies to 
raise awareness of MSME ﬁnancing and guarantee programs.

<!-- PAGE 94 -->

Empowering Filipinos through digitalization

Digitalization plays a crucial role in enabling ﬁnancial inclusion. The Paleng-QR Ph 
Plus  (PQRPh)  program  continued  to  drive  digital  payments  adoption  among 
vendors, transport drivers, microentrepreneurs, and informal workers. By the end 
of  December  2025,  the  program  covered  over  900  LGUs  through  local  policies, 
launch events, and endorsements from the Metro Manila Council, as well as several 
provinces and Regional Development Councils.

In November 2025, the BSP and the Department of Information and Communications 
Technology (DICT) signed a memorandum of agreement to institutionalize satellite 
and infrastructure support for digitalization initiatives, including PQRPh Plus.

The BSP, Citi Foundation, and the Microfinance Council of the Philippines, Inc. (MCPI) 
also  held  the  4th  Digital  Financial  Inclusion  Awards.  The  event  recognized  
17  microentrepreneurs  and  microﬁnance  institutions  for  successfully  adopting 
digital solutions.

Advancing ﬁnancial health

In  2025,  the  Consumer  Empowerment  and  Financial  Health  Core  Group  was 
established under the interagency FISC. The core group serves as the coordinating 
body for advancing consumer ﬁnancial health and empowerment, driving strategy 
formulation,  policy  and  program  alignment,  capacity  building,  multisectoral 
collaboration, and evidence-based monitoring.

At the regional level, the Philippines elevated ﬁnancial health as a Priority Economic 
Deliverable  in  preparation  for  its  2026  ASEAN  Chairship.  This  initiative  addresses 
emerging  challenges  such  as  rising  household  debt  and  the  need  for  greater 
ﬁnancial resilience. It aims to position ﬁnancial health as a shared regional priority 
by promoting information exchange, coordinated action, and common measurement 
approaches while respecting ASEAN diversity and country-speciﬁc contexts.

66 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

<!-- PAGE 95 -->

Economic and ﬁnancial learning

The BSP actively promotes public awareness of essential economic and ﬁnancial 
principles,  deepening  understanding  of  their  relevance  to  the  broader  economy 
and everyday life. A ﬁnancially informed public is better equipped to make sound 
decisions—supporting  savings,  asset  building,  responsible  credit  use,  and  overall 
ﬁnancial well-being and ﬁnancial health. These efforts also reinforce trust in BSP 
policies;  contribute  to  long-term  price  and  ﬁnancial  stability;  and  encourage  the 
adoption of safe, efﬁcient, and inclusive payment and settlement systems.

Conducting economic and ﬁnancial learning sessions

Through the Economic and Financial Learning Program (EFLP), the BSP delivers 
timely  and  relevant  learning  activities  to  various  stakeholder  groups.  The  EFLP 
includes both BSP-initiated and client-requested lectures for schools, government 
agencies, private institutions, and other organizations.

In  2025,  the  BSP  conducted  1,692  economic  and  ﬁnancial  learning  sessions, 
reaching  125,826  participants  through  in-person,  virtual,  and  hybrid  platforms.65 
Topics ranged from basic financial literacy—such as budgeting, saving, and consumer 
protection—to more advanced subjects, including credit and debt management, 
insurance, banking operations, digital payments, and currency integrity.

To strengthen delivery capacity, the BSP conducted training-of-trainers programs 
for both partner and non-partner institutions nationwide. In 2025, these programs 
equipped over 399 trainers with the skills to deliver ﬁnancial education effectively.66

Financial Education Stakeholders’ Congress

The  BSP  hosted  the  2025  Financial  Education  Stakeholders’  Congress  on  
17–18 November  2025  at  the  BSP  Assembly  Hall  in  Manila.  The  event  convened  
3,422  ﬁnancial  education  advocates  from  public  and  private 
institutions, 
international organizations, development partners, civil society, and the academe.

<!-- PAGE 96 -->

Strengthening and expanding financial education partnerships

The BSP advanced its financial education mandate through expanded partnerships, 
scaled program rollouts, and sustained capacity-building efforts. These initiatives 
strengthened institutional ownership while broadening nationwide reach.

Partner-led activities were central to delivering ﬁnancial education across priority 
sectors, including students, teachers, OFWs, government employees, MSMEs, and 
rural communities. Partner programs reached 11,262,351 individuals during the year, 
comprising over 10.3 million students and nearly a million OFWs.

In 2025, six new partnerships were established with the Boy Scouts of the Philippines, 
Department  of  Human  Settlements  and  Urban  Development  (DHSUD),  DICT, 
MCPI, RBAP, and TransUnion. These collaborations further institutionalized financial 
education across sectors.

National Strategy for Financial Education

The  BSP  is  leading  the  development  of  the  Philippines’  National  Strategy  for 
Financial Education (NSFE). The strategy aims to establish a coherent, nationally 
anchored  framework  to  strengthen  ﬁnancial  literacy,  capability,  and  well-being, 
particularly among priority and vulnerable segments.

As part of the pre-formulation phase, the BSP undertook analytical groundwork to 
inform strategic direction and priorities. This included key informant interviews on 
the supply side, conducted alongside the WB, to assess existing ﬁnancial education 
initiatives, institutional roles, and implementation gaps among partner agencies.

Efforts to inform the development of the NSFE were complemented by peer learning 
exchanges  with  fellow  central  banks  that  have  established  national  financial 
education  strategies,  including  Otoritas  Jasa  Keuangan  (Indonesia’s  Financial 
Services Authority) and Bank Negara Malaysia.

Developing economic and ﬁnancial learning materials

The BSP develops tailored economic and ﬁnancial learning resources in accessible 
and engaging formats, including infographics, short videos, digital tools, and print 
materials. These are distributed through print, ofﬁcial websites, and social media 
platforms.

In 2025, the BSP produced 118 learning materials designed to simplify key personal 
ﬁnance  and  economic  concepts.  These  were  made  freely  and  widely  available 
through the PisoLit and EkoLit social media channels, as well as the BSP E-Learning 
Academy  (BELA).  Topics  covered  include  practical  ﬁnancial  management,  digital 
literacy, scam prevention, consumer protection, and core economic topics.

68 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

<!-- PAGE 97 -->

BSP E-Learning Academy

As  a  key  digital  learning  platform,  BELA  recorded  102,764  module  enrollments 
during the year. This reach was further expanded through a partnership with the 
Technical Education and Skills Development Authority (TESDA), which generated 
an  additional  94,221  enrollments,  bringing  total  participation  across  individual 
electronic learning modules to 196,985.

Building  on  this  momentum,  the  BSP  initiated  strategic  collaborations  and 
platform  enhancements  to  improve  BELA’s  reach,  scalability,  and  effectiveness. 
These efforts focus on strengthening digital infrastructure, exploring complementary 
platforms  to  broaden  public  access,  and  enhancing  the  quality  and  efﬁciency  
of  module  delivery.  BELA  modules  are  also  being  integrated  into  partner-led 
capacity-building initiatives nationwide.

Protect Your Money campaign

The  BSP  not  only  safeguards  ﬁnancial  consumers  but  also  promotes  greater 
awareness  of  their  rights  and  responsibilities.  As  chair  of  the  Financial  Sector  
Forum (FSF)67 Consumer Protection and Education Committee, the Bank launched 
the refreshed Protect Your Money (PYM) campaign on 17 June 2025, with the tagline 
“I am a smart ﬁnancial consumer.”

The  campaign  encourages  informed  ﬁnancial  decision-making  and  equips  the 
public  to  guard  against  scams  and  fraud.  It  delivers  practical  guidance  through 
social media, print, radio, short message service (SMS), and ATM messaging.

Campaign activities in 2025 included distributing 50,000 refreshed PYM posters to 
BSP ofﬁces and partner agencies, releasing social media reels on common scams, 
and  launching  a  dedicated  PYM  webpage  on  the  BSP  website.  These  initiatives 
reinforce the safe use of digital ﬁnancial services, build public trust, and advance 
the BSP’s broader mandate of ﬁnancial literacy and consumer protection.

Driving public awareness across priority areas

Foreign exchange

The BSP continually strengthens the dissemination of FX regulations and statistics 
through  targeted  initiatives.  In  2025,  nine  in-person  brieﬁngs  were  conducted  
for  banks  and  industry  associations,  covering  FX  market  rules,  reforms,  and 
liberalization measures.

Complementing  these  efforts,  the  BSP  regularly  posted  FAQs,  infographics,  and 
informational  videos  on  FX  regulations  on  its  website  and  social  media  pages.68 
These  initiatives  aimed  to  ensure  that  stakeholders  clearly  understand  FX  rules, 
thereby promoting compliance.

To broaden public awareness and guide international travelers, the BSP conducted 
a  virtual  brieﬁng  on  cross-border  currency  transfer  rules  for  the  Civil  Aeronautics 
Board (CAB) and airline companies. The Bank also coordinated with CAB to include 
related advisories on ofﬁcial platforms and in-ﬂight announcements.

The BSP hosted study visits from the Bank of Lao PDR and the Central Bank of Sri

Consumer welfare

To  reinforce  safeguards  against  ﬁnancial  scams,  the  BSP  mounted  extensive 
information  campaigns  to  raise  awareness  of  the  AFASA.  The  central  bank 
partnered  with  the  Bankers  Institute  of  the  Philippines  (BAIPHIL)  to  conduct 
webinars on the intricacies of the law and its implementing rules.

AFASA  was  also  included  as  a  core  topic  in  16  media  information  sessions 
conducted nationwide. BSP resource persons further promoted the law through 
engagements  with  key  stakeholders,  including  government  agencies,69  ﬁnancial 
industry associations,70 and international and private organizations.71

The BSP also expanded its initiatives to educate the public about consumer rights 
under  the  Financial  Consumer  Protection  Act  (FCPA)  and  the  responsibilities  of 
account  owners.  Consumer  protection  messages  were  disseminated  through 
social  media  posts  and  posters  on  the  FCPA  and  the  consumer  redress  process. 
BSP  staff  also  served  as  resource  persons  on  media  programs  and  encouraged 
public participation by inviting consumers to share their insights in various forums.

Currency integrity and counterfeit detection

Extending its ﬁnancial literacy efforts, the BSP promotes public understanding of 
currency security and counterfeit detection to support the safe and informed use 
of  cash.  In  2025,  23  learning  events  on  these  topics  were  conducted,  including  a 
technical brieﬁng on the First Philippine Polymer Banknote Series, two specialized 
training sessions for law enforcement ofﬁcers, and 13 Know Your Money sessions for 
cash  handlers.  The  BSP  also  hosted  the  2025  COINversations  lecture  series, 
covering numismatics, currency, and related advocacies.

These  learning  events  were  reinforced  by  broader  public  information  efforts 
delivered through media interviews, briefings, social media content, and educational 
videos. Collectively, these initiatives reﬂect the BSP’s intensiﬁed regional outreach 
in  promoting  central  banking  awareness,  currency  integrity,  ﬁnancial  literacy, 
digital payments, and the new banknote series.

<!-- PAGE 99 -->

Consumer protection

The BSP remains committed to safeguarding the welfare of ﬁnancial consumers 
amid the continued digitalization of financial services. Anchored on core regulations 
on  ﬁnancial  consumer  protection  and  fraud  prevention,72,73  the Bank  advanced  a 
comprehensive agenda in 2025. Key initiatives included issuing the implementing 
rules  for  AFASA,  enhancing  the  BSP  Online  Buddy  (BOB)  chatbot  for  consumer 
assistance, strengthening complaints reporting for banks, and intensifying public 
communication programs.

Implementing fraud controls and safeguards

The  central  bank  fast-tracked  the  implementation  of  AFASA,  which  requires 
stronger  fraud  safeguards  and  more  efﬁcient  fraud  detection,  monitoring,  and 
response systems from BSIs. The law also authorizes the BSP to obtain information 
on  ﬁnancial  accounts  suspected  of  involvement  in  scams,  enabled  by  speciﬁc 
exemptions from bank secrecy and data privacy laws.74

To operationalize AFASA, the BSP issued three implementing circulars in 2025:

•  Circular No. 1213 requires BSIs to deploy robust, automated, and real-time 
fraud detection systems, along with stronger security measures, improved 
tools. 
transaction  veriﬁcation,  and  proactive  consumer  protection 
Compliance with these requirements is monitored as part of BSP supervision.

•  Circular  No.  1214  sets  out  clear  procedures  for  investigating  ﬁnancial 
accounts potentially involved in fraud and for disclosing information to law 
enforcement  and  relevant  government  agencies.  The  Circular  shall  be 
complemented by information-sharing agreements to be executed with the 
Cybercrime Investigation and Coordinating Center (CICC), National Bureau 
of  Investigation  (NBI),  Anti-Money  Laundering  Council  (AMLC),  DOJ,  PNP, 
and  SEC.  These engagements  shall  focus  on  defining  the  secure  and 
appropriate use of ﬁnancial account information in investigating scams and 
building cases against perpetrators.

•  Circular No. 1215 provides rules for the temporary holding of disputed funds 
and coordination among BSIs. Institutions may hold disputed funds for up 
to  30  days,75  based  on  ﬁndings  from  their  own  or  another  BSI’s  fraud 
management system or on complaints from account owners. BSIs are also 
required  to  establish  an  industry  protocol  for  effective,  coordinated,  and 
timely transaction veriﬁcation.

72 Republic Act No. 11765 (Financial Products and Services Consumer Protection Act)

<!-- PAGE 100 -->

Expanding consumer redress and dispute resolution

Consumer engagement with BSP redress channels expanded signiﬁcantly in 2025. 
Under  its  consumer  assistance  mechanism  (CAM),  the  Bank  received  and 
processed  over  120,000  new  complaints.  This  represents  a  72.6-percent  rise  from 
70,112  in  2024.  Complaint  volumes  have  risen  steadily  over  the  years,  from  19,181 
in 2021.  The  upward  trend  reﬂects  greater  public  awareness  of  the  BSP’s  CAM 
channels,  wider  adoption  of  digital  platforms,  and  increased  media  attention  on 
the FCPA and AFASA.

Complaints that are not resolved through facilitative CAM processing are elevated 
to mediation. Referrals for mediation rose by 48.9 percent, from 710 in 2024 to 1,057 
in 2025. In total, the BSP handled 1,188 mediation cases, including 131 carried over 
from the previous year. Of the 876 concluded cases, 409 were successful,76 144 failed,77 
and 323 were terminated for various reasons.78

Meanwhile,  the mediation  success  rate  declined  from  84.4 percent  in  2024  to 
74.0 percent  in  2025.79,80  This  drop  may  be  attributed  to  stronger  consumer 
awareness of alternative remedies, such as adjudication or court action.

Subject  to  certain  conditions,  complainants  may  pursue  adjudication.  In  2025, 
68 formal complaints were handled, including 12 carried over from 2024. Of these  
cases,  11  were  decided,81  20  were  dismissed,82  and  37  remained  pending  as  of 
31 December 2025.  These  redress  mechanisms  supported  fair,  impartial,  and 
orderly  resolution  of  consumer  disputes,83  consistent  with  the  BSP’s  mandate 
under the FCPA.

BSP Online Buddy

BOB, the BSP’s AI-enabled chatbot, remained the primary gateway for consumers 
to  escalate  complaints  to  the  central  bank.  As  the  primary  intake  channel  
under its CAM, the BSP implemented system enhancements to BOB to improve 
responsiveness  and  functionality.  Major  backend  upgrades 
improved  data 
extraction and categorization, resulting in a more robust complaints database and 
granular reporting. These developments allow the BSP to better utilize complaints 
data for policy formulation and evidence-based supervision.

76  “Successful”  cases  refer  to  those  where  both  parties  entered  into  a  settlement  agreement,  or  where  matters 
were sufﬁciently clariﬁed during mediation, and both parties agreed to terminate the proceedings as successful. 
77 “Failed” cases refer to those where the parties were unable to reach an amicable settlement. 
78 “Terminated” cases are those discontinued for other reasons, such as a party’s lack of interest in pursuing the 
mediation or the existence of factual or legal issues that cannot be resolved through mediation, among others. 
79 Success rate = “Successful” mediations/(Total of “Successful” and “Failed” mediations) 
80 The success rate excludes cases classified as “terminated” for other reasons, which are neither successful nor failed. 
81 “Decided” cases are those in which the BSP exercised jurisdiction and resolved the case on the merits. 
82 “Dismissed” cases are those disposed of without decision on the merits, including cases where complainants 
failed to comply with formal or essential requirements to commence adjudication, failed to appear without a valid 
reason at the scheduled preliminary conference, or fell outside the scope or jurisdiction of BSP adjudication, or 
were resolved through settlement, compromise, or withdrawal of the complaint.

and Adjudication of Cases in the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas)

<!-- PAGE 101 -->

Strengthening complaints reporting for banks

Drawing on the FCPA’s implementing rules,84 the BSP revised the requirements for 
the  Banks’  Consolidated  Complaints  Report  (BCCR)  in  2025.85  The  BCCR  is  a 
standardized  regulatory  report  that  consolidates  data  on  the  nature,  volume, 
handling,  and  resolution  of  customer  complaints,  which  inform  BSP  policy  and 
supervisory activities.

The  revised  BCCR,  now  integrated  into  the  Prudential  Reporting  Innovation  and 
Monitoring  Engine  (PRIME),86  introduces  standardized  taxonomies,  validation 
controls, and extensible markup language-based submissions. To ensure its uniform 
and effective adoption, the BSP conducted capacity-building sessions for banks in 
October and November 2025.

Developing the Consumer Protection Supervision Framework

The BSP continued developing the Consumer Protection Supervision Framework, 
which  adopts  a  risk-based  approach  to  assessing  BSI  activities  that  affect 
consumer  experience.  Selected  elements  were  tested  during  an  onsite 
examination  of  one  DGB  and  one  non-bank  EMI.  The WB  provided  advisory 
support  and  facilitated  workshops  to  help  align  the  framework  with  globally 
accepted  supervisory  principles.  These  efforts  support  the  BSP’s  objective  of 
embedding a culture of consumer protection across the ﬁnancial system.

<!-- PAGE 102 -->

Strategic communication

The  BSP  leverages  strategic  communication  to  enhance  public  understanding, 
build trust, and sustain confidence in its policies and initiatives. Clear and purposeful 
engagement  underpinned  its  core  mandates  in  2025.  BSP  communications 
centered on reinforcing transparency, broadening access, and upholding integrity 
in an increasingly digital ﬁnancial landscape.

Delivering impact through strategic campaigns

The BSP develops communication strategies to guide campaign implementation. 
These strategies deﬁne campaign objectives, identify target audiences, determine 
appropriate communication channels, and clarify key messages. They also outline 
speciﬁc  initiatives  designed  to  achieve  desired  outcomes  and  help  target 
audiences understand BSP policies and programs.

In 2025, the BSP crafted communication strategies to:

•  promote  the  10-Piso  ASEAN Coin  in  line  with  the  Philippines’  ASEAN

Chairship in 2026;

•  promote the Philippine Festivals Commemorative Coin Series, supporting

the central bank’s role in culture and nation-building; and

•  expand  the  Check-Protect-Report  (CPR)  initiative—the  BSP’s  ﬂagship 
cyber  hygiene  campaign—by  onboarding  key  government  agencies, 
including  fellow  regulators,  the  state broadcaster,  and  the  thrift  and 
rural banking communities.

As part of its stakeholder engagement efforts, the BSP piloted the Student Tours 
at  the  BSP  Hub.  These  tours  familiarized  students  with  the  central  bank’s  role 
in the economy, bringing together almost 500 participants from major universities 
in  Metro  Manila.  The  tours  featured  guided  visits  to  BSP  exhibits  and  facilities, 
as well as discussions on the Philippine Polymer Banknote Series and its currency 
security features.

Promoting arts and culture

The  BSP  actively  promotes  cultural  heritage  and  fosters  artistic  appreciation  by 
expanding access to its collections and cultural products. Efforts in 2025 included 
curated exhibitions, commemorative currency releases, and strategic partnerships 
that enriched public appreciation of the Philippine artistic and numismatic legacy.

Corporate product management

The  BSP  generated  strong  interest  in  its  corporate  products,  selling  a  total  of  
189,931 pieces in 2025. Commemorative notes and coins accounted for the largest 
share,  reflecting  sustained  demand  for  numismatic  items  that  celebrate  the 
country’s history and identity.

74 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

<!-- PAGE 103 -->

To  increase  visibility,  the  BSP  established  13  pop-up  stores  at  various  corporate 
events  organized  in  partnership  with  key  stakeholders.  These  pop-ups  provided 
convenient  venues  for  the  public  to  access  BSP  products,  including  books  and 
merchandise.

Museum exhibition and collections stewardship

Two  major  exhibitions  were  launched  during  the  year.  From  Barter  to  Money 
(Numismatic Evolution) at Museo BSP in the BSP Hub offers an immersive look at 
the  evolution  of  money  and  trade,  attracting  7,248  visitors  from  July  to

from the BSP Collection was mounted at the National Museum of the Philippines 
(NMP) from August to November 2025, featuring 36 pieces from the central bank.

In  addition,  139  paintings  were  loaned  and  exhibited  across  seven  galleries  at  
the  NMP.  A  further  39  pieces  from  the  BSP’s  precolonial  gold  collection  were

two  BSP  paintings  were  exhibited  at  the  Bank  Negara  Malaysia  Museum  and  
Art Gallery in Kuala Lumpur.

<!-- PAGE 104 -->

Digital transformation

Digital transformation at the BSP positions technology as an enabler of its core mandates. 
In  2025,  technology  enhanced  service  reliability,  supported  better  decision-making, 
and strengthened institutional resilience across ﬁnancial, supervisory, and operational 
central banking functions.

These benefits were matched by measurable cost efficiencies. The BSP generated over 
₱30.0 million in cost savings and cost avoidance by improving network use, optimizing 
cloud resources, and retiring unused systems. Gains in cloud efficiency had a 52.3-percent 
increase  in  resource  utilization  and  ₱1.9 million  in  savings  from  eliminating  idle  assets. 
Greater  reliance  on  platform  reuse  and  in-house  development  further  reduced 
dependence on external service providers while accelerating project delivery.

Optimizing internal processes

Digital transformation supported the delivery of 10 major information technology (IT) 
projects  in  2025.  Shared  enterprise  platforms  improved  integration,  efﬁciency,  and 
responsiveness across the BSP.

Core  systems—including  the  Enterprise  Resource  Planning,  Credit  Information 
Management  System,  and  other  markets  and  payments  platforms—streamlined 
operations. Governance, risk, and security systems, alongside the continued alignment 
with international cybersecurity standards, strengthened oversight.

Digital  transformation  has  extended  further  to  human  resource  (HR)  management. 
Rolled out  in  May  2025,  “HR Connect”  modernized  HR  operations  by  providing 
employees  with  easier  access  to  HR  information,  automating  key  processes,  and 
strengthening 
the  BSP’s 
commitment to a more agile and innovative workforce.  Enterprise data, analytics, AI, 
and  cloud  platforms  also  enabled  data-driven  decisions.  Collaboration  tools  like 
Microsoft 365 and enterprise AI solutions have enhanced work efﬁciency.

talent  management.  This  advancement  supports

Improving operational resilience and reliability

Operational resilience remained a key focus of digital transformation. The BSP adopted 
a “resilience-by-design” approach, ensuring that systems can sustain operations during 
disruptions and recover quickly when needed.

Cybersecurity and governance frameworks maintained a Level 2 (“manageable”) rating, 
recording zero major incidents. Staff awareness improved, reﬂected by better detection 
and reporting of phishing attempts and fewer cases of compromised accounts. The BSP 
also fully complied with key privacy and security requirements and successfully passed 
surveillance audits, reinforcing robust governance and data protection practices.

Scaling responsible artiﬁcial intelligence adoption

The BSP’s AI program progressed from isolated experimentation to a more structured, 
scalable, and governed ecosystem. Priority use cases entered testing and pilot stages 
across  supervision,  markets,  payments,  research,  and  other  operations.  This  progress 
was  backed  by  a  stronger  foundation,  including  integrated  AI  tools,  secure  cloud 
systems, and centralized data platforms. The BSP AI Sandbox and Project Mabini also 
provided safe, controlled environments for the responsible development and testing of 
AI solutions while supporting alignment with global central banking practices.

76 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 105 -->

Engaging stakeholders through digital channels

BSP Mobile App

Enhancements  to  the  features  and  functionalities  of  the  BSP’s  mobile  application 
continued  in  2025.  Key  updates  include  an  enhanced  BOB  chatbot,  improved  user 
interface for statistical content and the BSI Directory, and integration with other BSP 
platforms  and  systems  such  as  the  BSP  Store,  BELA,  BSP  Careers  Portal,  and  BSP 
Acquired Properties. The mobile app also introduced interactive screens, including the 
Trivia Game, the First Philippine Polymer Banknotes Series features preview, and the 
Layag virtual museum gallery.

In  addition  to  push  notiﬁcations,  the  app  now  offers  widgets  for  faster  access  to  the 
reference exchange rate bulletin, BSP announcements, and press releases. Users can 
also personalize their experience by selecting preferred BSP services or sections in the 
“Quick Access” feature and choosing foreign currencies in the “Exchange Rates” section.

BSP website

In July 2025, a major redesign of the BSP website was launched to enhance the user 
interface and user experience. Improvements included easier access to key rates, press 
releases, and public advisories, as well as streamlined search, menus, sections, and pages.

New  features  also  included  the  Release  Calendar,  which  highlights  important  
BSP  events,  and  the  “Monetary  Policy”  section  that  consolidates  relevant  reports, 
stance brieﬁngs, meetings, press releases, and speeches.

Driving sustainability through operational efﬁciency

The  BSP  views  sustainability  as  both  an  environmental  objective  and  a  catalyst  for 
transformation.  By  improving  processes  and  accelerating  digitalization,  the  Bank 
reduces  resource  use  and  its  operational  footprint  while  enhancing  timeliness, 
accuracy, and service delivery.

to  streamline

In  support  of  this,  the  BSP  has  implemented  robotic  process  automation  (RPA) 
tasks.  
initiatives 
These  processes  are  executed  by  computer  programs,  or  “bots,”  which  can  run  on 
demand,  on  a  schedule,  or  in  response  to  speciﬁc  triggers.  RPA  delivers  quick, 
high-impact gains by improving speed, efﬁciency, and accuracy.

The initiative generated substantial beneﬁts, including a 40–100 percent reduction in 
full-time  equivalent  requirements  for  selected  processes.  It  has  also  led  to  potential 
savings  on  supplies, 
reduced 
computational  and  encoding  errors,  more  efﬁcient  service  delivery,  and  higher 
customer satisfaction. Currently, 11 RPA bots are deployed across the BSP.87

improved  coordination  and  communication,

Ultimately, the outcomes achieved in 2025 establish a strong foundation for continued 
digital  transformation.  With  enhanced  resilience,  stronger  governance,  maturing  AI 
capabilities, and a future-ready workforce, the BSP is well-positioned to sustain reliable 
service delivery while responsibly advancing innovation in support of its mandate.

87 These include the BSPMail bot, which reduced email processing time by 50.0 percent by automatically responding 
to common inquiries using templated responses.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 77

---

<!-- PAGE 106 -->

Human resource and organization development

In 2025, the BSP underscored its commitment to operational excellence, employee 
development,  and  social  responsibility.  Through transformative  initiatives—from 
modernizing  human  resource  systems  to  promoting  workplace  excellence, 
continuous learning, and community engagement—the central bank reinforced its 
position  as  a  progressive  and  resilient  organization.  These  accomplishments  not 
only strengthened the BSP’s internal capabilities but also solidiﬁed its standing as 
a respected leader in the ﬁnancial sector, both locally and internationally.

Championing continuous learning and thought leadership

Recognizing  the  need  for  agile,  accessible,  and  future-ready  learning,  the  BSP 
leveraged  digital  technology  and  AI  to  complement  traditional  training  and 
support continuous employee upskilling. The BSP launched the LinkedIn Learning 
Experience  on  the  BSP  Education  in  the  Digital  Generation  (EDGe)  platform, 
providing  employees  with  on-demand  access  to  world-class,  AI-enabled  learning 
resources that support self-directed learning, professional growth, and organizational 
capability building.

In  its  commitment  to  excellence,  the  BSP  remained  an  active  thought  leader, 
serving as a resource speaker in numerous forums and sharing best practices with 
peer  institutions.  Among  its  local  partners  were  Landbank  of  the  Philippines, 
Development Academy of the Philippines, Polytechnic University of the Philippines, 
Department of Social Welfare and Development (DSWD), and DILG. International 
counterparts included the State Bank of Pakistan, Bank of Thailand, Bank Negara 
Malaysia, Deutsche Bundesbank, and the Asian Development Bank (ADB).

Promoting workforce wellness and workplace excellence

The BSP advanced employee well-being and engagement by integrating physical 
and  mental  wellness  into  its  business  strategy.  Through  the  WellMind@Work 
program, the Bank strengthened organizational performance and talent retention, 
building a more resilient and adaptive workforce.

This focus on employee wellness is complemented by efforts to promote workplace 
excellence.  The  BSP  engaged  a  third-party  assessor  to  validate  its  Organization 
Climate Survey for the year, resulting in its “Great Place to Work” Certiﬁcation on 
24 November 2025. With 92.0 percent of employees afﬁrming the BSP as a great 
place  to  work,  well  above  the  65.0 percent  benchmark  in  the  Philippines,  this 
recognition establishes the central bank as an employer of choice and reﬂects its 
commitment to fostering a supportive and inclusive work environment.

The BSP’s reputation as a leading employer was further afﬁrmed by its recognition 
by the Chartered Financial Analyst (CFA) Society Philippines as one of the “Top 10 
Employers  of  CFA  Charterholders  in  the  Philippines.”  This  honor  highlights  the 
Bank’s appeal to highly skilled professionals and its standing in the ﬁnancial sector.

Beyond  its  core  functions,  the  BSP  contributed  to  social  development  through 
meaningful  partnerships  and  outreach  programs.  These  included  support  for 
healthcare, child welfare, education, and environmental sustainability.

78 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 107 -->

Risk management and compliance

Proactive  risk  management,  business  continuity,  and  compliance  practices 
underpin the BSP’s institutional resilience. In 2025, the Bank strengthened these 
measures by adopting a more anticipatory, practical, and value-adding approach, 
focusing on risks that materially affect its mandates and strategic objectives.

The  BSP  sustained  the  organization-wide  implementation  of  its  Risk  Culture 
Program  and  integrated  environmental  considerations  into  its  Enterprise  Risk 
Management Framework by adopting an ESG risk appetite. BSP units developed 
key risk indicators for major and mandate-related risks. Risk and control assessments 
were consolidated and analyzed through a network science approach to identify 
risk propagation pathways, top enterprise risks, and key risk drivers.

Key  milestones  included  the  launch  of  a  Governance,  Risk,  and  Compliance 
Enterprise  Risk  Management  System,  which  consolidates  bank-wide  risk  and 
compliance information to enable end-to-end analysis. Another is the development 
internal  guidelines  that  operationalize  the  revised  Standard  Operating 
of 
for  Philippine  Holidays,  establishing  a  clear,  consistent,  and 
Procedures 
risk-informed  framework  for  holiday  operations  that  is  aligned  with  business 
continuity, operational resilience, and accountability requirements.

Under the Compliance Management System, compliance reviews and testing were 
conducted  in  eight  priority  ofﬁces.  The  BSP  also  underwent  independent 
assessments, including ISO 9001, ISO 45001, ISO 14001, and ISO/IEC (International 
Electrotechnical  Commission)  27001.  Compliance  with  the  SWIFT  (Society  for 
Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunications) Customer Security Controls 
Framework was likewise validated, bolstering the BSP’s cybersecurity posture.

implemented  data-driven  and 
To  enhance  operational  resilience,  the  BSP 
evidence-based  initiatives,  including  probabilistic  modeling  of  work  suspensions 
under  various  climate  scenarios,  an  agent-based  evacuation  simulation,  and  the 
Integrated  Business  Continuity  Exercise  (IBCE).  The  IBCE  afﬁrmed  the  central 
bank’s ability to recover and sustain critical operations within established recovery 
time objectives. Other enhancements cover IT resilience and remote work capabilities 
against operational disruptions.

The  BSP  also  actively  participated  in  local  and  international  training  programs; 
conferences; study visits; and exercises on risk management, cybersecurity, and the 
use, management, and development of AI. BSP ofﬁcers also served subject-matter 
experts in regional and international forums.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 79

---

<!-- PAGE 108 -->

Legal services

The  BSP  played  an  instrumental  role  in  the  passage  of  several  priority  measures 
under  the  Legislative-Executive  Development  Advisory  Council  (LEDAC).  Among 
these are:

•  RA No. 12145 or the Economy, Planning, and Development Act,  
•  RA No. 12214 or the Capital Markets Efﬁciency Promotion Act, 
•  RA No. 12216 or the National Housing Authority Act, 
•  RA No. 12254 or the E-Governance Act, and  
•  RA No. 12312 or the Anti-Philippine Offshore Gaming Operations Act of 2025.

Moreover,  the  BSP  coordinated  with  the  Philippine  Congress  to  advance  priority 
central  banking  measures,  including  the  proposed  amendments  to  the  Law  on 
Secrecy of Bank Deposits and the BSP-supported Digital Payments Act. The BSP 
also  prepared  position  papers  on  numerous  LEDAC  priority  bills,  including  the 
Cybersecurity  Act,  amendments  to  the  Cooperative  Code,  and  the  Cooperative 
Banking Act.

In  enforcing  banking  rules  and  regulations,  the  BSP  secured  convictions  in 
744 criminal  cases  ﬁled  against  28  directors,  trustees,  ofﬁcers,  operators,  and 
employees,  as  well  as  13  borrowers  of  16  BSFIs.88    The convictions  were  obtained 
from  22  trial  courts  across  the  country,  involving  violations  of  RA No. 8791  
(The  General  Banking  Law  of  2000); RA No.  7653 (The  New  Central  Bank  Act),  
as amended; the Revised Penal Code; and other relevant banking laws, rules, and 
regulations.

88 This included 14 closed banks and two MSBs.

80 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 109 -->

Asset management

The  BSP  sustained  efforts  to  expedite  the  disposal  of  its  acquired  properties. 
From 06 to 08 October 2025, the Bank participated in the National Housing Shelter 
Month celebrations held simultaneously in Legazpi City, Albay and Koronadal City, 
South  Cotabato.  This  continued  involvement  reﬂects  the  BSP’s  commitment  to 
ﬁnancial inclusion and support for the government’s efforts to provide affordable 
housing for Filipinos. By offering properties and reliable, transparent information to 
potential  homeowners,  the  BSP  fosters  integrity-driven  communities,  promotes 
innovation, and strengthens economic resilience.

As  of  31  December  2025,  the  total  book  value  of  BSP-acquired  and  other  real 
properties stood at ₱11.4 billion.

Through various schemes, the BSP disposed of 424 properties with a total net book 
value  of  ₱702.9 million 
for  an  aggregate  purchase  price  of  ₱2.3 billion. 
This generated  an  estimated  net  income  of  ₱1.6 billion,  inclusive  of  ₱0.4 million  
in other income and net of administration cost and broker’s commission, totaling 
₱58.3 million.  Compared  with  2024,  net  income  in  2025  increased  by  ₱1.1 billion. 
This increase is largely due to the sale of a property in Dasmariñas City, Cavite for 
₱1.9 billion, which alone generated ₱1.2 billion in net income.

Total  collections  from  sales  contract  receivables  reached  ₱405.0 million,  while 
interest  income  for  the  period  amounted  to  ₱94.5 million.  Moreover,  the  BSP 
earned  ₱231.5 million  in  miscellaneous  income,  largely  from  realized  proﬁts  on 
properties sold on installment from previous years amounting to ₱195.7 million.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 81

---

<!-- PAGE 110 -->

Comptrollership management

The BSP reinforces public conﬁdence in digital payments by setting the standard 
and leveraging digital platforms in its own transactions and disbursements. In 2025, 
the Bank implemented the Uniﬁed Payment Solution Platform (UPSP) to improve 
the efﬁciency and effectiveness of its digital payment processes.

As  a  centralized  system,  the  UPSP  integrates  multiple  payment  methods  and 
gateways,  including  QR  codes,  debit  and  credit  cards,  mobile  wallets,  and 
point-of-sale  terminals.  This  integration  enables  a  seamless  and  more  efﬁcient 
payment experience for BSP stakeholders.

Meanwhile,  the  BSP  declared  additional  cash  dividends  for  calendar  year  2024 
amounting to ₱18.9 billion. The full amount was remitted to the NG on 04 July 2025.

82 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

---

<!-- PAGE 112 -->

Financial results

Balance sheet

As  of  end-December  2025,  the  BSP  recorded  total  assets  of  ₱8.0  trillion, 
an increase of  ₱193.0 billion  (2.5 percent)  from  ₱7.8 trillion  in  the  previous  year.89 
The expansion  was  primarily  attributed  to  the  6.0-percent  y-o-y  increase  in 
international reserves. This was largely due to the positive revaluation of the BSP’s 
reserve assets, particularly gold holdings amid higher gold prices, as well as income 
from  investments.  However,  this  upward  trend  was  partially  tempered  by  a 
17.3-percent  decline  in  domestic  securities,  which  fell  from  ₱1.1 trillion  in  2024  to 
₱929.8 billion in 2025.

In  terms  of  composition,  BSP  assets  consisted  of  international  reserves totaling 
₱6.5  trillion  (80.9  percent)  and  domestic  securities  totaling  ₱929.8  billion 
(11.6 percent).

<!-- PAGE 113 -->

Income statement

In  2025,  the  BSP  registered  total  revenues  of  ₱272.5 billion,  largely  composed  of 
interest income from international reserves.90 This total was 9.3 percent lower than 
the  previous  year.  Over  the  same  period,  total  expenses  fell  by  11.1 percent,  
from  ₱226.6 billion  to  ₱201.4 billion.  Interest  expenses  remained  the  largest  cost 
component, accounting for about 64.0 percent of total expenditures.

The  decline  in  revenues  was  largely  attributable  to  weaker  trading  gains,  which  
fell by ₱33.0 billion compared with the previous year. This was primarily due to a 
₱58.5-billion reduction resulting from price ﬂuctuations in gold transactions with 
foreign  ﬁnancial  institutions.  The  decline  was  partly  tempered  by  a  ₱19.3-billion 
improvement in foreign securities, as trading losses of ₱22.9 billion in 2024 reduced 
to ₱3.6 billion in 2025.

Despite lower revenues, the BSP’s net income increased to ₱130.1 billion. This was 
higher  by  ₱12.1 billion  (10.3 percent)  than  ₱118.0 billion  in  the  previous  year. 
The improvement  was  mainly  driven  by  higher  net  realized  FX  gains  of 
₱59.0 billion, an increase of ₱14.9 billion from ₱44.1 billion in 2024, largely reﬂecting 
spot FX transactions.

The  impact  of  weaker  revenues  was  further  mitigated  by  lower  expenses, 
particularly  interest  expenses  on  open  market  operations  and  government 
deposits, which declined by ₱33.5 billion. These savings were partly offset by higher 
operating  expenses,  which  rose  by  28.0 percent,  from  ₱41.6 billion  in  2024  to 
₱53.2 billion in 2025.

90 Figures were based on preliminary and unaudited ﬁnancial data.

Technical Notes:
1. Balance of payments (BOP) statistics from 2005 onwards are based on the International Monetary Fund's Balance of Payments and International Investment Position Manual  (6th edition).
2. Financial account, including reserve assets, is calculated as the sum of net acquisitions of financial assets less net incurrence of liabilities.
3. Balances in the current and capital accounts are derived by deducting debit entries from credit entries.
4. Balances in the financial account are derived by deducting net incurrence of liabilities from net acquisition of financial assets.
5. Negative values of net acquisition of financial assets indicate withdrawal/disposal of financial assets; negative values of net incurrence of liabilities indicate repayment of liabilities.
6.

A negative growth rate in the net balance of the financial account and its components (i.e., direct, portfolio, and other investments) indicates higher net financial inflows or lower net outflows during the period relative to the year-
ago level. Conversely, a positive growth rate suggests lower net financial inflows or higher net outflows.

7.

Overall BOP position is calculated as the change in the country's net international reserves, less non-economic transactions (revaluation and gold monetization/demonetization).

Alternatively, it can be derived by adding the current and capital account balances less financial account plus net unclassified items.

8. Net unclassified items serve as an offsetting account to the overstatement or understatement in either receipts or payments of the recorded BOP components vis-à-vis the overall BOP position.
9. Data on deposit-taking corporations, except the central bank, consist of transactions of commercial and thrift banks and offshore banking units (OBUs).

Details may not add up to total due to rounding.
r

Revised to reflect data updates from official data sources and post-audit adjustments

.

Rounds off to zero

The historical and latest releases are available at: https://www.bsp.gov.ph/SitePages/Statistics/Statistics.aspx.

3.3

Technical Notes:
1. Balance of payments (BOP) statistics from 2005 onwards are based on the International Monetary Fund's Balance of Payments and International Investment Position Manual  (6th edition).
2. Financial account, including reserve assets, is calculated as the sum of net acquisitions of financial assets less net incurrence of liabilities.
3. Balances in the current and capital accounts are derived by deducting debit entries from credit entries.
4. Balances in the financial account are derived by deducting net incurrence of liabilities from net acquisition of financial assets.
5. Negative values of net acquisition of financial assets indicate withdrawal/disposal of financial assets; negative values of net incurrence of liabilities indicate repayment of liabilities.
6.

A negative growth rate in the net balance of the financial account and its components (i.e., direct, portfolio, and other investments) indicates higher net financial inflows or lower net outflows during the period relative to the year-
ago level. Conversely, a positive growth rate suggests lower net financial inflows or higher net outflows.

7.

Overall BOP position is calculated as the change in the country's net international reserves, less non-economic transactions (revaluation and gold monetization/demonetization).

Alternatively, it can be derived by adding the current and capital account balances less financial account plus net unclassified items.

8. Net unclassified items serve as an offsetting account to the overstatement or understatement in either receipts or payments of the recorded BOP components vis-à-vis the overall BOP position.
9. Data on deposit-taking corporations, except the central bank, consist of transactions of commercial and thrift banks and offshore banking units (OBUs).

Details may not add up to total due to rounding.
p

Preliminary

.

Rounds off to zero

The historical and latest releases are available at: https://www.bsp.gov.ph/SitePages/Statistics/Statistics.aspx.

2025 BSP Annual Report I 10(cid:1487)

Innovation. Inclusion. Integrity.
ELI M. REMOLONA, JR.
Governor

2025 was a year of shifting narratives.
The first half carried the momentum of 2024. Inflation kept easing,
hitting a record low of 0.9 percent in July. That gave the Bangko
Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) room to keep the key policy rate at
4.5 percent. Growth held close to its strong 2024 pace.
The second half told a different story. Government reforms tied to
the flood control controversy slowed public infrastructure spending.
As uncertainty spread, business and consumer sentiment softened.
Growth eased to 4.4 percent for the full year. Inflation stayed below
target, averaging 1.7 percent, which helped support investments.
The good news is that the banking system remained in robust health.
Banks were well-capitalized and were flush with liquidity. The banking
system continued to support lending and economic activity.
That resilience was no accident. Years of prudent supervision and
regulation ensured that our banks were in a good position to absorb
shocks.
Even as conditions grew more challenging, the BSP pressed forward.
Alongside our core mandates, the BSP continued to pursue initiatives
that reflect our commitment to innovation, inclusion, and integrity.
ii | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Innovation
Good policy needs good and timely information. In 2025, the BSP
stepped up its use of artificial intelligence (AI) to better track a rapidly
changing environment. We employed large language models to
measure sentiment and monitor trade flows. These allowed us to
formulate a more data-driven monetary policy that is ahead of the
curve.
In payments, the BSP advanced the adoption of ISO 20022.
This is a global standard that makes both domestic and cross-border
transactions more transparent and secure. We also made progress
in cross-border payments through the incorporation of Nexus Global
Payments in Singapore. Once fully operational, Project Nexus will
link instant payment systems of member countries. This will enable
seamless cross-border transactions.
We also strengthened cybersecurity. The BSP developed and
tested the Cybersecurity Incident Response Plan for PhilPaSSplus,
our real-time gross settlement system. During the year, the system
processed a record ₱601.3 trillion in transactions at a 99.9 percent
efficiency rate. Meanwhile, transactions on InstaPay, the low-value
instant payment system, surged to 4.7 billion worth ₱11.6 trillion.
2025 BSP Annual Report I iii

Inclusion
More Filipinos now have access to financial services than ever before.
Account ownership among Filipino households increased to
85.0 percent. Beyond gaining access to digital financial services,
Filipinos also became more confident in using them in daily life.
The Paleng-QR Ph Plus Program expanded to more than 900 local
government units. This brought the benefits of digital payments to
market vendors, transport drivers, microentrepreneurs, and informal
workers across the country.
But access is only a starting point. The BSP elevated financial health
as a Priority Economic Deliverable (PED) for the 2026 ASEAN Summit,
which is chaired by the Philippines.
Integrity
Integrity remained the foundation of trust in the financial system.
In February, the Philippines exited the Financial Action Task Force’s
anti-money laundering grey list. This made it easier and cheaper
for businesses and individuals, especially overseas Filipino workers,
to remit funds. We also exited the high-risk jurisdiction lists of the
European Union and the United Kingdom.
In September, to further strengthen the integrity of the financial
system, the BSP set new rules for withdrawals. The rules are
designed to curb money laundering and other criminal activity while
minimizing impact on businesses and individuals with legitimate
and regular transactions. We supported this by strengthening the
standards for external auditors and raising reporting requirements for
non-bank financial institutions such as pawnshops and money service
businesses.
iv | 2025 BSP Annual Report

In August, responding to growing public concern, the BSP required
banks, electronic wallet operators, and other BSP-supervised financial
institutions to remove links to online gambling sites.
With these measures, we ensure that as financial services become
more accessible and innovative, the system remains credible and
worthy of public trust.
Looking ahead
The economy is now navigating new headwinds. The Middle East
conflict early in 2026 pushed inflation higher, prompting the BSP to
raise rates.
Fortunately, we remain on solid footing. Inflation is rising from a
low base. Our banks also remain strong, ready to absorb shocks and
sustain lending.
None of this was achieved alone. Our stakeholders and partner
agencies all played their part. So did the Monetary Board and every
BSP officer and staff.
Our combined efforts put us in a good position to weather the current
challenges. I am confident that continued collaboration will enable
us to navigate these conditions and further strengthen the Philippine
economy and the financial system for the benefit of all Filipinos.
2025 BSP Annual Report I v

WHO WE ARE
About the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
The BSP is the country’s independent central monetary authority mandated to promote
price stability, help maintain stability of the financial system, and oversee the country’s
payments and settlements system.
Section 20, Article XII, 1987 Philippine Constitution
The Congress shall establish an independent central monetary authority…
[which] shall provide policy direction in the areas of money, banking, and credit.
It shall have supervision over the operations of banks and exercise such regulatory
powers as may be provided by law over the operations of finance companies and
other institutions performing similar functions.
Section 1, Article 1, Chapter 1, Republic Act No. 7653
(The New Central Bank Act) as amended by Republic Act No. 11211
The State shall maintain a central monetary authority that shall function and
operate as an independent and accountable body corporate in the discharge
of its mandated responsibilities concerning money, banking, and credit. In line
with this policy, and considering its unique functions and responsibilities, the central
monetary authority established under this Act, while being a government-owned
corporation, shall enjoy fiscal and administrative autonomy.

2025 BSP Annual Report I vii

BSP Monetary Board Members (from left to right): Ralph G. Recto, Romeo L. Bernardo, Jose L. Querubin,
Governor Eli M. Remolona, Jr., Rosalia V. De Leon, Benjamin E. Diokno, and Walter C. Wassmer
The BSP’s Legal Mandate
Legal Basis of Establishment and Legal Mandate
The BSP is the central bank of the Republic of the Philippines. Section 20,
Article XII of the 1987 Constitution mandated Congress to establish an
independent central monetary authority tasked to provide policy direction
in the areas of money, banking, and credit. Pursuant to this constitutionally
mandated responsibility, Congress established the BSP through the
enactment of Republic Act No. 7653 in 1993.
Powers and Functions
As the Philippines’ central monetary authority, the BSP is responsible,
among others, for:
• maintaining price stability conducive to a balanced and sustainable
growth of the economy and employment;
• promoting and maintaining monetary stability and the convertibility of
the peso;
• promoting financial stability by closely working with the national
government, the Department of Finance, the Securities and Exchange
Commission, the Insurance Commission, and the Philippine Deposit
Insurance Corporation;
• overseeing the payments and settlements system, including
critical financial market infrastructures, to promote sound and
prudent practices; and
• promoting broad and convenient access to high quality financial
services for the interest of the general public.
viii I 2025 BSP Annual Report

The BSP is also tasked to perform the following functions:
Liquidity management. The BSP formulates and implement monetary policy
aimed at influencing money supply, consistent with its primary mandate of
maintaining price stability.
Currency issue. The BSP has the exclusive power to issue the national currency.
All notes and coins issued by the BSP are fully guaranteed by the
government and are considered legal tender for all private and
public debts.
Lender of last resort. The BSP extends discounts, loans, and advances to
banking institutions for liquidity purposes.
Financial supervision. The BSP supervises banks and exercises regulatory and
examination powers over non-bank institutions performing quasi-banking
functions, money service businesses, credit granting businesses, and payment
system operators.
Management of foreign currency reserves. The BSP seeks to maintain
sufficient international reserves to meet any foreseeable net demands for
foreign currencies to preserve the international stability and convertibility of
the Philippine peso.
Determination of exchange rate policy. The BSP determines the exchange
rate policy of the Philippines. Currently, it adheres to a market-oriented foreign
exhange rate policy to ensure orderly conditions in the market.
Bond issue. As part of monetary policy, the BSP may issue, place, buy, and sell
freely negotiable evidences of indebtedness.
Banker of the government. The BSP functions as the banker,
financial advisor, and official depository of the government, its political
subdivisions and instrumentalities, and government-owned and -controlled
corporations.
In 2019, over 25 years after the BSP was established, Republic Act No. 11211 was signed to
amend the BSP Charter. This law implemented reforms that align the BSP with global
standards, improve its operations, and enhance its policy-making that responds to
financial markets and the economy.
The supervisory and regulatory powers of the BSP are further enhanced by expanding the
coverage of institutions under the Bank’s supervision to include money service businesses,
credit granting businesses, and payment system operators.

2025 BSP Annual Report I xi

From strategic direction
to disciplined execution:
Orchestrating
the BSP 2024–2029
Enterprise Strategy
Following the launch of the BSP Enterprise Strategy in 2024, the Bank’s priority in 2025 was
clear: translate strategic direction into disciplined, coordinated execution across all operating
units. This required converting strategic priorities into measurable, shared commitments;
maintaining organizational alignment; and establishing systematic review mechanisms to
track progress and address emerging gaps.
Effective strategy orchestration keeps the organization moving as one. It strengthens
accountability, supports timely decision-making, and advances the BSP’s objectives of
fostering innovation, promoting financial inclusion, and upholding institutional integrity.
xii I 2025 BSP Annual Report

Turning strategy
into coordinated action
Strategy execution begins with effective cascade. The BSP Enterprise Strategy is
translated into clear priorities, measurable outcomes, and adequately resourced initiatives.
This enables every sector and office to align work programs with institution-wide goals.
Such alignment ensures that interdependent initiatives move together and performance
targets remain consistent across units.
In 2025, the BSP strengthened this “line of sight” from enterprise priorities to sectoral plans
and individual unit contributions. The Bank enhanced linkages between the enterprise
strategy and sectoral operational plans, supported operating units throughout the planning
cycle, and reinforced governance mechanisms for reviewing priorities, resource allocation,
and results. The highlights of the year include:
adopting a common planning framework across sectors,
improving clarity of priorities and accountability for results;
aligning Annual Work Programs with enterprise and sectoral
priorities, strengthening links between plans, budgets, and
expected results
enhancing strategy governance through clearer guidelines
and advisories, standardizing planning, review, prioritization,
and monitoring processes; and
deepening strategy communication and engagement,
equipping execution teams with a common understanding to
support consistent implementation across the Bank.
2025 BSP Annual Report I xiii

Strengthening strategy delivery
through project management
discipline
Project management discipline is fundamental to strategy delivery. It provides structure
to complex, interdependent work by clarifying accountabilities, identifying risks early, and
aligning project delivery with intended outcomes.
In 2025, the BSP strengthened project discipline by developing new Guidelines on
Post-Implementation Review and Project Quality Assurance. These guidelines set clearer
standards for assessing project outcomes and ensuring compliance with established
processes. Management oversight was reinforced through enhanced Project Status Reports,
which improved visibility of milestones, risks, and critical dependencies.
To build institutional capability, the BSP expanded engagement across project teams
through the Enterprise Project Management Roadshow. The Bank also strengthened
peer learning through the Project Managers Community of Practice and Interests Circle.
These platforms supported knowledge sharing, coordination, and problem-solving
across teams. With stronger standards, improved reporting, and better collaboration,
the BSP advanced a more coherent, institution-wide approach to project delivery in support
of its enterprise strategy.
Keeping strategy on track
Strategy remains relevant only through active management. This requires consistent
monitoring, structured reviews, and timely course correction.
In 2025, the BSP strengthened enterprise-wide strategy monitoring through a more
structured cadence of engagements and formal review forums. These platforms validated
performance, identified bottlenecks and emerging risks, and confirmed corrective
actions—ensuring that performance discussions remained timely, evidence-based, and
results-oriented.
Reporting standards were reinforced through improved validation of performance
submissions from Strategy Execution Teams (SETs). Consolidated insights were presented
in the Annual and Mid-Year Strategy Performance Reports for the Monetary Board (MB),
providing clear, decision-ready information. To maintain strategic oversight, regular briefings
between the MB and SETs were also conducted, deepening leadership alignment and
sustaining execution momentum.
Together, these enhanced monitoring frameworks and clearer feedback mechanisms
position the BSP to maintain strategic focus and deliver measurable outcomes across the
institution.
xiv I 2025 BSP Annual Report

Driving performance and
measurable outcomes
What gets measured gets improved. Monitoring performance allows the BSP to assess
what works, identify emerging gaps, and reinforce accountability.
In 2025, the BSP made steady progress in its strategic objectives. Monetary policy
formulation improved through stronger use of technology, research, and analytical tools.
Initiatives to strengthen systemic risk oversight and crisis management progressed as
planned. Capital market reforms gained traction, supported by preparatory work for
the possible inclusion of Philippine bonds in the J.P. Morgan index. Sustainability efforts
advanced through the establishment of dedicated environmental, social, and governance
offices and the integration of climate considerations into policy analysis. Research and
collaboration expanded and exceeded targets, resulting in more timely and evidence-based
decision-making.
The BSP also strengthened its institutional foundations. The rollout of the enhanced
Enterprise Human Resource System (EHRS) improved key processes and generated early
efficiency gains. Data management and cybersecurity enhancements progressed alongside
the automation of regulatory workflows and pilot testing of artificial intelligence (AI)
tools. Stakeholder engagement also improved through the expansion of digital platforms
and strategic partnerships.
Looking ahead, the BSP will focus on accelerating key deliverables and strengthening
critical enablers—particularly in digital infrastructure, performance management,
and talent development—to sustain progress and translate plans into measurable outcomes.
The Bank remains committed to fostering a culture of collaboration, accountability,
and innovation in advancing its strategic objectives.

xviii | 2024 BSP Annual Report

How we were in 2025: The global and domestic
macroeconomic landscape
Global economic developments
Global growth proved resilient in 2025 despite elevated trade tensions and policy
uncertainty. Favorable financial conditions and strong technology-related trade
supported activity, offsetting the drag from higher United States (US) tariffs.1
The International Monetary Fund (IMF) estimated global gross domestic product
(GDP) growth at 3.3 percent, while the World Bank (WB) placed it at 2.7 percent.
Growth was supported by inventory buildup, strong investor risk appetite, and
rising artificial intelligence (AI) investments.2
Global financial markets reflected this resilience. The Bank for International
Settlements (BIS) observed compressed credit spreads and elevated equity
valuations, particularly among large technology firms. These conditions signaled a
strong risk appetite but also increased the risk of abrupt market corrections.3
Figure 1
World Gross Domestic Product Growth
at constant prices; in percent

World Bank – Global Economic Prospects (January 2026)
e – estimates
p – projections

2 World Bank. (2026). Global Economic Prospects – January 2026.
https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/entities/publication/bb904ec6-730f-4dd9-b1af-ad3153ee1616

2 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

The year began with expectations of a soft landing. Inflation was still above-target
in most economies, but labor markets normalized, and financial conditions
remained accommodative. However, broad-based US tariff increases in Q2 2025
triggered a sharp reassessment of risk. Equity markets declined, sovereign yields
became volatile, and the US dollar initially weakened. Fiscal concerns and doubts
over central bank independence amplified market reactions.
Although trade tensions partially eased in the second half of the year, uncertainty
persisted. Global financial conditions remained broadly supportive, but asset
performance diverged. Sovereign yields were mixed across markets, while most
equity indices recovered. Meanwhile, large technology firms continued to
outperform, underscoring the concentration of stock market gains.
Global activity held up amid easing trade tensions and still-favorable financing
conditions. The IMF estimates that global growth slowed to 2.4 percent in Q3 2025,
as stronger-than-expected performance in some economies offset weaknesses
in others. Technology-related trade continued to support overall trade volumes,
mitigating the impact of softer demand in other sectors.
Looking ahead, global growth is expected to remain broadly stable. The IMF
projects global output to expand by 3.3 percent in 2026 and 3.2 percent in 2027.
The WB projects growth at 2.6–2.7 percent over the same period. Advanced
economies (AEs) are expected to expand modestly amid structural constraints and
moderating demand.4 Emerging market and developing economies are seen to
continue outpacing advanced peers, although elevated debt burdens and tighter
external financing conditions constrain several low-income economies.5
Risks to the global outlook for 2026 and 2027 remain tilted to the downside.
Asset valuations and investments appear sensitive to shifts in expectations
surrounding AI-driven productivity gains. Renewed trade or geopolitical tensions
could tighten financial conditions, disrupt supply chains, raise inflation, and
weaken trade flows. At the same time, faster technological diffusion and sustained
policy coordination could lift potential growth.
4 The IMF forecasts average growth in AEs at 1.8 percent in 2026 and 1.7 percent in 2027. In the US, output is
projected to grow by 2.4 percent in 2026, supported by fiscal spending and a less restrictive monetary stance,
before easing to 2.0 percent in 2027. Growth in the euro area is seen at 1.3 percent in 2026 and 1.4 percent in 2027,
aided by higher public spending in select states despite persistent structural constraints.
5 The IMF expects output to rise slightly above 4.0 percent in 2026–2027, while WB estimates indicate a gradual
easing from 4.2 percent in 2025 to around 4.0–4.1 percent. China and India are expected to remain key growth
drivers, while many low-income and frontier markets face weaker prospects due to elevated debt and tighter
external financing conditions.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 3

Philippine economic developments
Growth softens amid weaker domestic demand.
The Philippine economy grew by 4.4 percent in 2025, slower than the 5.7-percent
growth in 2024. Weaker outturns in the second half of the year weighed on
full-year performance. Domestic demand softened notably due to a contraction
in capital formation, particularly in public construction, and more moderate
household consumption. On the supply side, growth in the industry and services
sector decelerated.
Compared with regional peers, the Philippines posted relatively weaker growth
than Vietnam (8.0 percent), Malaysia (5.2 percent), Indonesia (5.1 percent), China
(5.0 percent), and Singapore (5.0 percent), but outpaced Thailand (2.4 percent).
Gross national income increased by 6.1 percent, moderating from 7.7 percent
in the previous year. This was primarily due to slower growth in net primary income,
which eased from 26.1 percent to 19.7 percent.
Figure 2
Gross Domestic Product and Gross National Income
at constant 2018 prices; annual growth rate in percent
Source: Philippine Statistics Authority
4 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Aggregate output and demand
Supply side
On the production side, the slowdown in growth was driven by weaker
performance in the industry and services sectors, partly offset by a rebound in the
agriculture, fishery, and forestry (AFF) sector. Industry growth, in particular, was
weighed down by weaker construction and manufacturing activity, reflecting
delays in government construction projects and subdued demand conditions.
The industry sector grew by 1.7 percent in 2025, marking its weakest performance
since 2020. It contributed 0.5 percentage point (ppt) to real GDP growth and
accounted for 28.3 percent of total output. The slowdown was largely driven by
weaker construction activity, following the ongoing infrastructure probe into flood
control projects.
Manufacturing growth also moderated due to weak demand and cost pressures,6
with output declines observed in the chemical and petroleum industries.
Meanwhile, mining and quarrying posted a modest recovery, supported by higher
coal production.
Figure 3
Gross Domestic Product by Industry
at constant 2018 prices; annual growth rate in percent
Source: Philippine Statistics Authority
6 This was reported in the S&P Global and Philippine Institute for Supply Management’s Purchasing Managers’
Index (PMI) for Philippine manufacturing.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 5

The services sector remained the primary driver of domestic economic growth
in 2025. It expanded by 5.8 percent, easing from 6.7 percent in 2024. Despite the
slowdown, the sector contributed 3.7 ppts to real GDP growth and accounted for
63.8 percent of total output.
However, growth in the services sector moderated, reflecting slower expansion
across key subsectors. Financial and insurance activities decelerated, as banks
adopted a more cautious lending stance amid weaker investor sentiment,
particularly in H2 2025.7
Professional and business services growth also softened, as global headwinds
weighed on outsourcing demand and tempered expansion in the information
technology and business process management (IT-BPM) industry.8 Tourism-related
activities likewise slowed, with softer growth in accommodation and food services,
as well as transportation and storage, amid subdued tourist arrivals.9
These slowdowns were partly offset by stronger growth in public administration
and defense, education, and human health and social work activities.
The AFF sector expanded by 3.1 percent in 2025, recovering from a 1.5-percent
contraction in the previous year. It contributed 0.2 ppt to overall GDP growth
and accounted for 7.9 percent of total output. The rebound was primarily driven
by higher crop production, particularly palay, corn, and sugarcane. This was
supported by more favorable post-El Niño and La Niña growing conditions and
improved farming practices. Livestock production also recovered, reflecting a lower
incidence of African Swine Fever and stronger biosecurity measures.
Demand side
On the expenditure side, growth was dampened by a decline in investment
and a moderation in household consumption. This was partly offset by stronger
government spending and a rebound in net exports (3.0 percent).
Household final consumption expenditure (HFCE) eased from 4.9 percent in 2024
to 4.5 percent in 2025, reflecting subdued consumer sentiment and the adverse
effects of weather disturbances. Despite the slowdown, household spending
remained the main driver of growth, contributing 3.3 ppts to real GDP growth
and accounting for 72.6 percent of total output.
7 Aligned with the slowdown in manufacturing (-9.4 percent) and other services (-5.8 percent), as well as the contraction
in construction (-5.4 percent), outstanding production loans in universal and commercial banks declined in 2025.
