LHS – left-hand side
RHS – right-hand side
Funding remained anchored on resident individuals at 48.9 percent and private
corporations at 31.7 percent. Deposits accounted for 73.3 percent of total assets
and 83.5 percent of total liabilities, reflecting banks’ reliance on stable core funding
and limited wholesale exposure.
Banks expanded their physical and digital footprint further.43 The total number of
banking offices increased from 13,384 in 2024 to 13,639 in 2025, consisting of
463 head offices and 13,176 other offices (branches and branch-lite units).
The network comprised 44 U/KBs (7,156 branches), 42 TBs (3,371 branches), 371 RCBs
(3,106 branches), and 6 DGBs.
The sustained expansion of physical and digital delivery channels boosted deposit
generation and broadened financial access. In parallel, the BSP continued to
promote operational resilience to ensure service continuity and maintain financial
integrity across platforms.
43 Apart from physical branches, more banks now deliver services through electronic banking (e-banking) and
other digital financial channels. As of end-December 2025, 24,394 automated teller machines (ATMs) are in
operation nationwide, while 139 banks offer BSP-approved e-banking facilities.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 33

Sufficient capital and liquidity buffers
The banking system remained strongly capitalized, with solo and consolidated
capital adequacy ratios (CARs) at 15.8 percent and 16.2 percent, respectively.44
Common Equity Tier 1 (CET 1) ratios stood at 14.8 percent and 15.2 percent,
respectively, indicating high-quality capital buffers. These ratios were well above
the regulatory thresholds. Total capital rose by 8.9 percent to ₱3.7 trillion,
strengthening loss absorption and balance sheet expansion.
Figure 11
Philippine Banking System’s Risk-Based Capital (Solo Basis)
value in billion pesos; ratios in percent
as of the end periods indicated
Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
CET1 – Common Equity Tier 1
RHS – right-hand side
These elevated buffers support sustained intermediation and investment in
innovation. However, the ongoing credit growth underscores the need for strong
internal capital generation and disciplined risk-taking to preserve capital adequacy
across the credit cycle.
Banks maintained prudent funding and leverage profiles. Reliance on market
funding was limited, with bonds and bills payable accounting for a modest share
of liabilities, indicating low refinancing risk. Leverage ratios stood at 9.1 percent
on a solo basis and 9.5 percent on a consolidated basis. This provided a strong
non-risk-based backstop to risk-weighted capital measures, which help mitigate
model risk and measurement limitations. Liquidity conditions were likewise sound.
The liquidity coverage ratio and net stable funding ratio both remained above
100 percent, while other banks exceeded minimum liquidity thresholds. Together,
these conditions underpin system confidence and financial stability.
44 U/KBs reported CARs of 15.6 percent on a solo basis and 16.0 percent on a consolidated basis as of end-December 2025.
34 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Solid interest-based earnings
Net profit rose by 3.6 percent to ₱405.6 billion. Growth was driven by the 9.1-percent
increase (₱136.4 billion) in interest income to ₱1.6 trillion, outpacing the 1.6-percent
increase (₱7.5 billion) in interest expense. This sustained profitability enables banks
to invest in innovation and improve the delivery of financial services. Preserving
earnings quality, however, requires disciplined pricing, prudent risk-taking, and
adequate provisioning.
Non-banks as conduits of financial access
BSP-supervised NBFIs played a critical role in deepening financial intermediation
and broadening access through digital payments and alternative credit channels.
Rapid expansion among electronic money issuers (EMIs) and payment service
providers, supported by wider adoption of e-banking, has advanced financial
inclusion by offering lower-cost and more accessible financial services to a wider
segment of Filipinos. Rising transaction volumes and greater interconnectedness
with banks and financial markets increased operational, cyber, fraud, and third-party
risks. This underscores the importance of strong governance, operational resilience,
and consumer protection in sustaining trust in digital finance.
Balance sheet conditions across key NBFIs remained broadly sound but uneven.
Non-banks with quasi-banking functions (NBQBs) posted moderate asset growth
at 5.2 percent and stronger lending growth at 8.6 percent. This was supported by
improving asset quality, with the NPL ratio at 4.0 percent, and stable profitability.
Non-stock savings and loans associations (NSSLAs) expanded steadily, with
member-focused lending supporting financial inclusion. Nonetheless, higher NPLs
of 8.0 percent highlighted the need for continued provisioning discipline and
robust credit risk management, even as loan loss coverage remained adequate.
Pawnshops and money service businesses (MSBs) provided critical last-mile access
to payment and remittance services. The high-volume and cash-intensive nature
of these operations calls for strong market conduct standards, anti-money
laundering and counter-terrorism financing (AML/CTF) compliance, and enhanced
consumer safeguards.
Financial stability policies and measures
The BSP remains committed to safeguarding financial stability while enabling
responsible innovation across the financial sector. In 2025, the Bank advanced a
set of prudential policy reforms and supervisory standards to address emerging
risks while preserving the safety, soundness, and efficiency of the Philippine
financial system.
Policy reforms and supervisory initiatives during the year were anchored on three
strategic priorities: (a) strengthening institutional stability, (b) promoting digital
transformation, and (c) advancing inclusive and sustainable finance. Through
calibrated regulations and close stakeholder engagement, the BSP promoted
financial system resilience, supporting inclusive and sustainable economic growth.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 35

Strengthening institutional stability and financial sector resilience
The BSP continually enhances governance standards, supervisory oversight,
and risk monitoring tools to reinforce financial sector integrity and resilience.
Governance reforms were implemented during the year to strengthen
accountability within BSP-supervised financial institutions (BSFIs), including
expanded grounds for disqualifying directors and officers involved in serious
regulatory violations.
In February 2025, the Philippines achieved a major milestone with its successful
exit from the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) grey list. This was followed by the
country’s removal from the high-risk jurisdiction lists of the EU and the UK,
reflecting significant enhancements in its AML/CTF framework. These developments
are expected to improve cross-border transactions and boost confidence among
international banking partners.
To mitigate illicit financial risks and improve transparency, the BSP introduced
regulations on large-value cash transactions. Cash payments exceeding ₱500,000
require enhanced due diligence at the customer level.45,46
The BSP revised the framework for selecting external auditors to strengthen
financial reporting and institutional oversight. Reporting governance standards for
NBFIs—including pawnshops and MSBs, such as remittance companies and FX
dealers—were also enhanced. These reforms promoted timely and reliable financial
reporting while aligning supervisory oversight with evolving financial technologies
and international standards.
Liquidity management policies were refined to support efficient financial
intermediation. In March 2025, the BSP reduced the reserve requirement ratios for
banks and selected NBFIs, releasing additional liquidity to facilitate credit
expansion and investment. Complementary adjustments to reserve requirements
for certain financial instruments streamlined regulatory requirements and promoted
capital market development.
The access of trust entities to BSP-issued securities through investment management
accounts was expanded to strengthen liquidity absorption and enhance monetary
policy transmission. Regulatory provisions governing financial intermediaries
and quasi-banking activities were also updated to clarify the application of the
“19-lender rule” and ensure consistency with evolving market practices.
To promote objective and transparent supervision, the BSP established an
independent review mechanism. This takes the form of a Board-level committee
tasked to review major supervisory issues arising from bank examinations while
preserving the BSP’s authority to enforce actions.
45 This was recalibrated to ₱1,000,000 based on the latest National Risk Assessment and findings from supervisory
reviews and engagements.
46 Enhanced due diligence measures may be applied on a proportionate or risk-based basis, depending on the
customer’s assessed risk profile, nature of business or operations, and transaction patterns.
36 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Driving secure financial sector innovation
The BSP continues to strengthen the financial system’s capacity to support digital
innovation while upholding robust risk management, operational continuity, and
consumer protection.
In February 2025, the BSP established the Financial Cyber Resilience Governance
Council to oversee the implementation of the Financial Services Cyber Resilience
Plan. The council brings together BSP officials and industry stakeholders to guide
sector-wide cybersecurity initiatives and monitor progress in reinforcing the financial
system’s defenses against cyber threats.
The BSP enhanced the regulatory framework to address financial fraud and
cyber-related risks. Implementing regulations under the Anti-Financial Account
Scamming Act (AFASA) were issued to improve the capacity of financial institutions
to detect and prevent fraudulent activities. Financial institutions are now required
to adopt advanced fraud monitoring tools, including real-time transaction monitoring,
behavioral anomaly detection, geolocation tracking, and device fingerprinting.
They must also provide “kill switch” mechanisms that allow customers to immediately
suspend compromised accounts.
Complementary regulations established procedures for financial account inquiries
and the temporary holding of funds involved in disputed transactions. These measures
enable more coordinated responses to fraud while safeguarding the rights of
legitimate account holders.
Regulatory policy priorities
The BSP is firmly committed to its mandate of safeguarding financial stability
through a calibrated supervisory approach and targeted regulatory reforms.
Its priority initiatives are anchored on two pillars: (a) fostering resilience and
(b) sustaining relevance.
Fostering resilience
This prudential policy direction focuses on strengthening the banking system’s
ability to absorb and manage a broad range of shocks—from cybersecurity threats
and financial disruptions to climate-related and other natural hazards—while
preserving overall financial stability.
Building on earlier issuances that enhanced the operational resilience of BSFIs,
the BSP will reinforce cybersecurity controls, establish crisis management and
resolution planning frameworks, implement a model risk management framework,
and advance climate adaptation-related policies. Basel III reforms for calculating
capital charges for operational and credit risks will also be adopted.
Collectively, these measures aim to ensure that banks remain financially sound,
operationally resilient, and well-positioned to respond to evolving risks.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 37

Sustaining relevance
The BSP recognizes that regulatory and supervisory frameworks must continually
evolve in response to innovation, digitalization, emerging risks, and shifts in
the macroeconomic landscape. To stay effective, these approaches must be
proportionate, forward-looking, and responsive, allowing banks to support
economic activity without compromising prudential standards.
Moreover, the BSP pursues reforms to modernize regulatory frameworks, reinforce
governance and financial integrity standards, and support the responsible
adoption of digital financial solutions. Key initiatives include rationalizing regulatory
reporting, strengthening AML/CFT requirements, updating credit and liquidity risk
guidelines, and introducing policies that facilitate the prudential expansion of
digital financial services.
Efforts to promote inclusive finance are integral to this agenda. These include
issuing guidelines for digital financial marketplaces, establishing frameworks for
the digital operations of smaller banks, and expanding access to Shari’ah-compliant
financial products.
By aligning regulatory priorities with emerging risks, the BSP seeks to sustain
confidence in the financial system and foster a banking sector that is stable,
relevant, and adaptable in advancing sustainable and inclusive economic growth.
Systemic risk management
Managing systemic risks is essential to advancing the BSP’s financial stability
mandate. In 2025, the central bank strengthened systemic risk oversight and
macroprudential policy design through deeper stakeholder collaboration and
analysis of linkages across banks, NBFIs, and non-financial corporations (NFCs).
As market developments heightened contagion risks from the real economy,
the BSP revisited its macroprudential toolkit to keep measures fit for purpose.
The BSP also continued to lead discussions within the interagency Financial
Stability Coordination Council (FSCC). This facilitated the identification of global
and domestic risks relevant to the Philippines and the delivery of initiatives in
support of the financial stability agenda.
Enhancing systemic risk surveillance and analysis
The BSP adopts a framework anchored on four primary risk channels—valuation
pressures, leverage in the non-financial sector, financial sector exposures, and
liquidity and funding risks—and two cross-cutting vulnerabilities: concentration
and interconnectedness. The quarterly systemic risk analyses (SRAs) and the
Financial Stability Report (FSR) were structured around this framework.
SRAs during the year focused on potential corporate systemic risks amid external
pressures, including shifts in US trade policy. In March 2025, the BSP, together with
the FSCC, released the 2024 FSR, highlighting the resilience of the Philippine
financial system despite global headwinds, geopolitical tensions, and policy shifts.
The report also underscored the need to continue monitoring risks related to asset
valuation, leverage, debt servicing, and the growing role of NBFIs.
38 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Strengthening systemic risk management models, metrics, and policies
The BSP enhances systemic risk management through integrated models, indicators,
and policy tools. Key measures in 2025 included:
• Assessing firm-level exposures and systemic risk buildup. Amid monetary
policy easing to support lending, the BSP developed a framework to assess
corporate debt exposures and track systemic risk buildup. The framework
monitors lending from a macroprudential perspective, supporting early
risk identification, and assesses the impact of domestic and cross-border
shocks on firm-level risks. Enhancements are being explored to facilitate
cross-country comparisons.
• Developing systemic risk indicators. The Systemic Vulnerability Indicator
consolidates macrofinancial data to monitor structural vulnerabilities across
key risk channels, providing a broad view of systemic risk conditions.
The Systemic Risk Sentiment Index complements this by capturing shifts in
market perception from economic and financial news, including risks not yet
reflected in traditional indicators. Together, these tools strengthen the BSP’s
forward-looking surveillance capabilities.
• Configuring macroprudential models. The BSP uses analytical tools to
enhance systemic risk assessment by linking macrofinancial conditions,
spillover channels, and bank balance sheet resilience. The Growth-at-Risk
framework estimates the distribution of future GDP growth under prevailing
macrofinancial conditions and signals the severity of potential adverse
economic outcomes.
The Diebold–Yilmaz Spillovers Index measures the peso’s historical sensitivity
to global shocks, based on the share of returns or volatility variance
attributable to foreign currency movements. Stress testing applies
macroeconomic and financial scenarios to banks’ loan portfolios to gauge
potential losses and capital impacts, informing assessments of system-wide
resilience and policy responses.
• Operationalizing the countercyclical capital buffer. In October 2025,
the BSP engaged the IMF through a technical assistance mission to
operationalize the countercyclical capital buffer (CCyB). The engagement
refined the framework’s methodology, calibration, governance, and
implementation. This marks a critical step toward a robust, forward-looking
CCyB framework to strengthen banking sector resilience against systemic
risks from credit cycles.
Priority areas for 2026 include adopting the positive neutral CCyB for banks,
operationalizing the corporate sector exposure framework for routine surveillance,
integrating NBFI data into systemic risk assessments, and developing a macro
stress testing methodology and framework.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 39

Advancing systemic risk crisis management
The BSP initiated the development of a Systemic Crisis Management Playbook to
establish a coherent approach to managing potential systemic events. It aims to
consolidate crisis management frameworks across BSP sectors and support the
2024–2029 BSP Enterprise Strategy, particularly Strategic Objective 2: Strengthened
Systemic Risk Oversight and Supervision.
The playbook outlines processes for continuous surveillance, timely escalation
and communication, coordinated interventions, intra-agency collaboration, and
post-event learning. Efforts to refine and harmonize systemic risk management
approaches across the BSP commenced in 2025. Activities in 2026 will focus on
integrating sector-specific frameworks, conducting sectoral consultations, and
presenting the playbook to the FSCC.
40 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Fostering a safe, efficient, and inclusive payments and
settlements system
Digital payments expansion through strategic innovation and
purposeful collaboration
Fast payments breach all-time high.
Digital payments in the Philippines accelerated sharply in 2025, underscoring the
continued shift toward real-time, cashless transactions by individuals and businesses.
Growth was anchored on the rapid expansion of InstaPay, which processed a
record-high of 4.7 billion transactions worth ₱11.6 trillion in 2025. This represents
increases of 230.9 percent in volume and 57.3 percent in value from 2024. The surge
reflects the mainstream adoption of account-to-account transfers for everyday
payments, supported by improved accessibility, reliability, and user trust.
Figure 12
InstaPay, QR Ph Person-to-Merchant, and InstaPay QR Transaction Volume
2018–2025
Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
The quick response (QR) code-based payment ecosystem, particularly QR Ph
person-to-merchant (P2M) and InstaPay QR for person-to-person (P2P) payments,
continued to support digital payments expansion in 2025. QR Ph P2M posted 2.5 billion
transactions amounting to ₱1.2 trillion, reflecting broader merchant acceptance and
rising consumer preference for low-friction retail payments. InstaPay QR contributed to
this momentum, with 183.3 million transactions valued at ₱926.1 billion. This corresponds
to a y-o-y growth of 246.9 percent in volume and 211.3 percent in value, driven by
increased P2P payments.
Meanwhile, the Philippine Electronic Fund Transfer System and Operations Network
(PESONet) remained the primary channel for higher-value transfers. It processed
117.2 million transactions worth ₱13.2 trillion in 2025, marking increases of 16.3 percent
in volume and 30.9 percent in value from the previous year. This underscores the role
of PESONet in corporate, payroll, and bulk payments. Taken together, the combined
transaction value of InstaPay and PESONet accounted for nearly 90.0 percent of the
nominal GDP in 2025.
Overall, the year marked steady progress in the digitalization of the retail payment
system. The scale and acceleration of InstaPay, combined with sustained PESONet
activity, reflected greater accessibility, efficiency, and trust in digital payment channels.
These gains were supported by continued investments in infrastructure, standards,
and governance initiatives led by the BSP and its partners.
42 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Strengthening the payments infrastructure for efficiency and interoperability
Sustained excellence of the Peso Real-Time Gross Settlement Payment System
The BSP continues to ensure the safe, efficient, and resilient operation of the Real-Time
Gross Settlement (RTGS) Payment System—the backbone of the Philippine digital
payments ecosystem. In 2025, the BSP’s fund transfer infrastructure, known as the
Philippine Payment and Settlement System Plus (PhilPaSSplus), achieved an overall
efficiency rate of 99.957 percent.
PhilPaSSplus facilitated the real-time execution of large-value payments between
financial institutions and the final settlement of retail payment clearing results within
fractions of a second. By enabling fast, affordable, and low-risk settlement of
time-critical and high-value transactions, it played an indispensable role in supporting
the stability of the country’s financial system.
Figure 13
Philippine Payment and Settlement System Transactions
volume in million transactions; value in trillion pesos
2021–2025
Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
PhilPaSSplus processed 1.7 million transactions with an aggregate value of ₱601.3 trillion, the
highest annual volume and value recorded since its launch in 2021.47 Transaction
volume grew by 9.0 percent y-o-y, primarily driven by USD/PHP FX and GS trades.
Market developments—including exchange rate swings mostly favoring the USD,
large-scale GS issuances, and improved liquidity following the BSP’s reduction of
reserve requirements and policy rates—bolstered trading activity.
47 PhilPaSS was rolled out in 2002, enabling highly automated operations with real-time or near real-time settlement
and enhanced system-generated reporting. In 2021, PhilPaSSplus was launched, upgrading the system with increased
settlement capacity and efficiency, stronger security, expanded access channels, improved interoperability, and more
comprehensive payment information.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 43

In terms of value, transactions grew by 15.8 percent y-o-y. This was largely attributed to
increased bank placements in the BSP’s ODF and faster turnover of wholesale funds
through the improved intraday settlement facility (ISF). This same-day repo facility
enables RTGS participants to settle transactions using BSP-sourced funds when
liquidity shortfalls arise.
In 2025, 16 participants were onboarded to the Peso RTGS Payment System, including
the country’s largest non-bank EMI, further advancing financial inclusion.
Figure 14
Philippine Payment and Settlement System Participants
2025
Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
Reinforced payments settlement risk framework and resilience measures
The BSP implemented targeted measures to bolster risk management and operational
resilience in payments settlement in 2025. These included the development and testing
of the peso RTGS’ Cybersecurity Incident Response Plan to enhance cyber preparedness.48
The BSP also streamlined its standard operating procedures for local or unscheduled
Philippine holidays and work suspensions. The changes ensure the continuous
operation of the country’s large-value peso payment system and reflect the Bank’s
capability to perform critical functions during public sector disruptions. As a result,
PESONet and check clearing services remain available even during these periods.
Further enhancements were made to the ISF to enable automatic availment once
participants have posted the required securities. Beyond simplifying the process,
this significantly reduced funding delays, minimized queued transactions, and
prevented payment gridlock, reinforcing the overall resilience, safety, and efficiency of
the RTGS System.
48 This initiative was undertaken in collaboration with the WB.
44 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

To support business continuity, the BSP entered into cooperation agreements with key
financial market infrastructures (FMIs) and retail payment clearing operators. These
arrangements ensure uninterrupted settlement processes and the seamless flow of
funds across financial institutions, businesses, and individuals.
The impact of these initiatives is reflected in the BSP’s biannual client satisfaction
survey for RTGS participants. In the 2025 survey, governance, system security, and
settlement efficiency received the highest scores, indicating strong participant trust in
the BSP as the operator of the Peso RTGS Payment System.
Project Agila: Exploring wholesale central bank digital currency capabilities
The BSP completed Project Agila, its wholesale central bank digital currency (CBDC)
initiative, in 2025.49 Through a proof-of-concept, the project confirmed
the technical feasibility of issuing central bank money on a programmable ledger and
enabling inter-institution fund transfers outside traditional operating hours. These
capabilities not only strengthen core payments infrastructure but also open
opportunities for innovation in securities settlement and cross-border transactions.
Drawing on the insights from Project Agila, the BSP envisions a future where wholesale
CBDCs transform financial markets and cross-border payments through greater speed,
efficiency, and trust. To realize this vision, the BSP will deepen its collaboration with
stakeholders and global experts to support the development of a more seamless,
digital, and interconnected Philippine economy.
ISO 20022: Standardizing payments messaging
The BSP gained global recognition as one of the pioneering central banks to
implement ISO (International Organization for Standardization) 20022 across all its
payment systems, beginning in 2021.
Building on this early adoption, the BSP is aligning all payment systems with the
Harmonized ISO 20022 Standard issued by the BIS Committee on Payments and
Market Infrastructures in October 2023. This move positions the BSP well ahead of the
global end-2027 target for cross-border payments.
On 20 November 2025, the BSP issued Circular No. 1223, requiring all retail payment
systems in the country to adopt ISO 20022. This global messaging standard enables
more seamless, efficient, and secure domestic and cross-border retail payments.
Its consistent implementation will improve transparency and speed up the resolution of
inquiries through richer transaction data.
Practical benefits include faster return of funds for failed credit transfers and access to
complete payment information. These enable payees to perform quicker and more
accurate post-payment processing, enhance transaction monitoring, and support
more effective customer redress mechanisms.
The BSP is currently developing the implementation rulebook and identifying the first
use case for harmonization. Full compliance is targeted within two years from the
Circular’s effectivity date.
49 A wholesale CBDC is a digital form of central bank money that could be used for large-value transactions across
financial institutions.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 45

Nexus: Broadening cross-border instant payments
In March 2025, Nexus Global Payments Ltd. (NGP) was incorporated in Singapore as a
company limited by guarantee to operationalize and manage the Nexus scheme.
This marked the transition of Project Nexus from a conceptual initiative to a live,
institutionally governed cross-border payments platform.
NGP was established by the five first-mover central banks: the BSP, Bank Negara
Malaysia, Bank of Thailand, Monetary Authority of Singapore, and Reserve Bank
of India. It operates as a not-for-profit entity with a public interest mandate and governs
the Nexus scheme. The scheme links domestic instant payment systems across
jurisdictions through a single connection per system, reducing technical complexity,
lowering integration costs, and enabling scalable multilateral expansion.
Bank Indonesia began its transition from “Special Observer” status and initiated
the formal process toward becoming an “Owner” of NGP by 2026. Meanwhile,
the European Central Bank continued its participation as a “Special Observer” and
advanced feasibility work for a potential connection to Nexus, subject to required legal
arrangements.50
With approximately 10.8 million Filipinos living and working overseas in 2024,51
enhanced connectivity across Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, India, and Indonesia
is expected to benefit many Filipino remitters and their beneficiaries through faster
fund transfers, improved transparency, and potentially lower transaction costs.
NGP also continues to engage with other jurisdictions, including those in the Middle
East and other parts of Asia, to expand network linkages.
Project Mandala: Reinventing cross-border flows compliance
On 8 October 2025, the BSP joined the kickoff of Project Mandala Phase II alongside
several central banks, including those of Australia, Singapore, Malaysia, India, and
Kuwait. Project Mandala is a BIS Innovation Hub initiative that aims to improve
cross-border payments and digital asset compliance by shifting key checks to
a cryptographically verifiable pre-validation framework. This approach reduces
transaction failures, duplication, costs, and delays.
Phase II focuses on scalable, privacy-preserving, and programmable solutions
that integrate with existing systems and support broader adoption. As part of
the Business and Policy Working Group, the BSP will contribute to defining
priority use cases, establishing implementation pathways, and engaging industry
partners to demonstrate an interoperable compliance engine across tokenized and
non-tokenized platforms.

network with the addition of Indonesia [Press release]. https://www.nexusglobalpayments.org/nexus-global-
payments-appoints-first-board-chair-and-expands-network-with-the-addition-of-indonesia
51 The Filipino Times. (2025, August 27). DFA: Over 10.8 million Filipinos lived abroad in 2024.
https://filipinotimes.net/latest-news/2025/08/27/dfa-over-10-8-million-filipinos-lived-abroad-in-2024

Diversifying digital payments use cases
Automated fare collection system
As part of the efforts to modernize public transportation, the Department of
Transportation (DOTr), in coordination with the BSP, approved G-Xchange Inc. (GCash)
to conduct a proof-of-concept for the automated fare collection system on the Metro
Rail Transit Line 3 (MRT-3). The pilot run was launched in July 2025, with the nationwide
rollout targeted for 2027.
The proof-of-concept enabled the deployment of three cashless rail fare payment
modalities: (a) Scan-to-pay using account-based ticket QR, (b) Tap card via EMV
(Europay, Mastercard, and Visa) debit and credit cards, and (c) Tap-to-pay with near field
communication-enabled mobile phones. These payment options improve service
efficiency by reducing reliance on stored-value card top-ups.
Interoperable Direct Debit payment scheme
The pilot run of the Direct Debit system, involving four participating banks,
commenced in December 2025. This interoperable payment service is intended to
benefit both billers and payors by streamlining collection processes and minimizing the
risk of incurring late fees or penalties. It also provides a more convenient and viable
alternative to bilateral auto debit arrangements and post-dated check warehousing.
Digital payment system security through robust oversight
and surveillance
Enforcing safeguards against online gambling
In response to the evolving online gambling landscape, and anchored on its authority
under Republic Act (RA) No. 11127, the BSP directed the suspension of in-app gambling
access in mobile payment apps and websites of BSP-supervised institutions (BSIs).52
Following this directive, the Philippine Amusement and Gaming Corporation reported
a significant decline—approximately 50.0 percent—in online gambling transactions in
the days immediately after electronic wallets delinked from gambling platforms.53
Demonstrating its commitment to robust oversight, the BSP issued 147 show cause
letters to various entities, a significant portion of which were related to addressing
concerns surrounding online gambling. These cited non-compliance with the BSP’s
directive on the suspension of in-app gambling access, as well as oversight lapses and
operational deficiencies.
Enhancing interagency collaboration
In July 2025, the BSP and the Bureau of the Treasury (BTr) formalized a strategic
partnership through the signing of a memorandum of understanding (MOU). The MOU
aims to strengthen the safety, efficiency, and resilience of the country’s core FMIs,
particularly those critical to GS trading and large-value fund transfers.
52 BSP Memorandum No. M-2025-029 (Suspension of In-app Gambling Access in Mobile Payment Apps and Websites)
53 Panti, L. T. (2025, August 20). PAGCOR: Online gambling transactions down 50 percent since e-wallets ban. GMA News.
https://www.gmanetwork.com/news/money/economy/956429/pagcor-online-gambling-transactions-down-50-sin
ce-e-wallets-ban/story
2025 BSP Annual Report | 47

As the first formal cooperation between the BSP and the BTr extending beyond
operational processes, the MOU reflects a shared commitment to managing systemic
risk, deepening the capital market, and aligning FMIs with international standards.54
It also provides for enhanced collaboration in information and knowledge sharing,
capacity building, technology enhancement, and whole-of-market initiatives, while
supporting joint preparations for external assessments such as the Financial Sector
Assessment Program (FSAP).55
Meanwhile, the BSP issued a joint memorandum circular with the Cooperative
Development Authority (CDA). This initiative streamlined regulatory oversight, clarified
supervisory roles, and facilitated the efficient onboarding of cooperatives into the
financial and payments system, marking a significant step toward greater institutional
coordination and regulatory effectiveness.
Advancing data-driven payments oversight, supervision, and surveillance
To support stronger and proactive payments oversight, the BSP initiated efforts to
transition toward a data-driven supervisory approach. Key initiatives included:
• capability building for select BSP personnel to establish a strong
foundation on AI use cases that would support supervisory functions;
• development of the Payments Oversight Data Management System to
enhance the timeliness and risk-based nature of supervisory actions by
leveraging improved data management capabilities; and
• scoping and structuring of an entity data repository to centralize
information on supervised entities, strengthen institutional knowledge,
and support supervisory activities.
Together, these initiatives lay critical groundwork for the BSP’s standardization of
supervisory processes.
Responsive currency management
In 2025, the BSP maintained stable and responsive currency management amid
moderating economic conditions. Strategic transformation initiatives strengthened
supply resilience and advanced operational modernization.
Currency demand
Amid slower economic activity and low inflation, currency in circulation reached
₱2.9 trillion. This reflects an annual growth rate of 6.4 percent, a deceleration from
11.3 percent in 2024.
54 This particularly refers to the Principles for Financial Market Infrastructures.

sector. It includes an evaluation of financial development and produces recommendations covering microprudential
and macroprudential aspects, as well as country-specific developmental needs.

2021–2025
Source: Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas
Annual growth in banknote withdrawals from the BSP slowed, rising by 6.2 percent
in value. Volume grew strongly at 12.9 percent, driven by higher-than-expected
withdrawals of 100-piso and 50-piso denominations, which rose by 25.3 percent and
44.3 percent, respectively.
The BSP demonstrated operational agility by realigning in-house production in
Q4 2025 toward lower denominations. This ensured a timely supply to fulfill banks’
higher demand and supported the day-to-day liquidity requirements of the public.
Total banknote deposits with the BSP increased by 16.2 percent in value and
12.1 percent in volume. Coin deposits grew significantly, rising by 23.2 percent in value
despite a slight decrease in volume. Most of the deposited coins were found to be fit.
Polymer banknotes continued to demonstrate superior durability relative to paper
banknotes. This reinforces the BSP’s transition to polymer and its commitment to a
more cost-efficient and sustainable currency lifecycle.
Fit deposits grew by 32.3 percent in 2025, up from 4.6 percent in 2024. This surge was
largely on account of the 35.2-percent increase in fit deposits of 1000-piso banknotes,
with polymer notes accounting for a 46.4 percent share. These trends reflect a high
return rate of clean and usable 1000-piso banknotes, supported by a 37.1-percent
increase in total fit withdrawals and a 19.5-percent decline in withdrawals of newly
printed 1000-piso notes.
Coin withdrawals decreased by 14.4 percent in value, largely due to a 21.1-percent drop
in demand for the highest coin denomination, the 20-piso coin. In contrast, withdrawal
volume rose by 1.4 percent y-o-y, reflecting higher demand for 25-sentimo and
5-sentimo coins.

Gold production
Since 2014, the BSP has consistently received the Responsible Gold Certificate from the
London Bullion Market Association (LBMA). This certificate indicates that gold bought
by the BSP through its gold buying stations is sourced legally and ethically, with due
regard for environmental and occupational health and safety standards.
The BSP also earned the Proactive Monitoring Certificate, signifying its capability to
perform gold assaying and casting in compliance with LBMA requirements, thereby
enabling its good delivery bars to be sold in the Loco London market.
Security documents and commemorative coin production
Beyond its role as the country’s sole issuer of currency, the BSP is also responsible for
producing critical security documents. In 2025, the central bank successfully fulfilled
the order of 7,360,000 pieces of judicial title forms for the Land Registration Authority.
Moreover, the BSP continues to advance its numismatic initiatives. It produced the
100-piso Philippine Festivals Silver Commemorative Non-Circulation Legal Tender
Coin Series, titled “Pista sa Pilipinas,” which was launched in December 2025.
To commemorate the Philippines’ ASEAN Chairship in the coming year, the BSP
designed the 10-piso ASEAN Commemorative Coin, highlighting the country’s
leadership and contributions to the region.
Strategic modernization and system resilience
The BSP implements strategic initiatives to strengthen currency management. In 2025,
these efforts focused on improving operational efficiency, mitigating supply chain risks,
and enhancing system resilience. The measures support adaptability to changing
economic conditions and leverage technology to improve transparency and oversight
across the cash cycle.
Supply chain security
To secure the currency supply chain, the BSP entered into international agreements
with Note Printing Australia, Bundesdruckerei GmbH, and Banque de France, including
arrangements for polymer banknote supply. The central bank also signed an MOU with
the Royal Canadian Mint to collaborate on various coin management initiatives,
technology exchange in gold refining and assaying, and commemorative coin programs.
50 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Operational efficiency
The BSP’s measures to improve operational efficiency include the 2025 Integrated
Currency Management Framework, which aligned strategic and operational priorities
for 2026. This was complemented by the institutionalized Lean Manufacturing–Total
Productive Maintenance program, which generated ₱495.8 million in validated annual
financial benefits while enhancing efficiency, governance, and resilience.
Meanwhile, the BSP approved the project charter for the Enterprise Currency
Management System (ECMS) to replace the legacy system. Targeted for
implementation by 2027, the ECMS will enhance real-time cash visibility, modernize
cash infrastructure, and strengthen overall cash operations. In March 2025, the BSP
deployed the enterprise resource planning system “HanaRise” to enhance its
production, facilities, and financial operations.
The BSP further advanced circulation efficiency through the completion of the Coin
Deposit Machine Project Phase I and the approval of its nationwide Phase II expansion.
From its launch in June 2023 to the end of its contract in June 2025, a total of ₱1.6 billion
worth of coins was collected. The Cash Service Alliance recirculated ₱336.8 billion in fit
currency, while ₱43.7 million in rebates, refunds, and incentives were paid to banks to
support responsible currency management.
International recognition
The BSP received the “Best New Banknote or Banknote Series” award from the
International Association of Currency Affairs 2025 Excellence in Currency Awards and
was named “Currency Manager of the Year” by Central Banking. These recognitions
highlight the BSP’s efforts to ensure a reliable supply of trusted and durable banknotes,
particularly through the introduction of the polymer banknote series.
Strengthened currency integrity and public confidence
Assessment of mutilated banknotes and coins
The BSP upheld its commitment to serving the public by determining the genuineness
and redemption value of currencies submitted for examination. In 2025, the volume of
severely damaged banknotes examined by the BSP declined by 16.1 percent. Paper
banknotes comprised 83.8 percent of claims, while polymer banknotes accounted for
the remaining 16.2 percent. The volume of examined mutilated coins also dropped by
25.8 percent. Most claims were from public submissions, reflecting continued reliance
on BSP mechanisms for addressing mutilated currency.

2025 BSP Annual Report | 51

Anti-counterfeiting activities
Documented counterfeit banknotes decreased by 17.1 percent y-o-y.56 This reduction
reflects the effectiveness of enhanced banknote security features that made
counterfeiting more difficult, alongside increased public awareness and expanded
training that strengthened counterfeit detection capabilities.
Paper banknotes comprised the majority of counterfeit banknote submissions at
97.7 percent. Polymer banknotes exhibited resilience, accounting for the remaining
2.3 percent. Since their release in 2022, polymer banknotes have represented only
0.6 percent of all recorded counterfeit banknotes.
In contrast, counterfeit coins increased but remained below one part per million (ppm) of
genuine coins in circulation. Most counterfeit coins were remnants from previous years,
primarily involving 5-piso coins from the BSP Coin Series. The enhanced security
features of the New Generation Currency, including the micro-printed details and
laser-engraved designs, effectively curbed counterfeiting attempts.
Table 3

Counterfeiting techniques predominantly involved inkjet printing, which accounted for
63.3 percent of documented cases. This was followed by laser printing at 33.5 percent
and altered security threads at 3.1 percent.
Geographically, reported counterfeit banknotes were highest in NCR (45.3 percent),
with Region IV-A or CALABARZON (11.3 percent) as a far second. Counterfeits were
frequently recovered in areas with high foot traffic, such as shopping malls
(33.9 percent), supermarkets (23.8 percent), and wet markets (17.6 percent).
Law enforcement and public engagement
The BSP, in collaboration with partner agencies, conducted five law enforcement
operations in 2025. Notable among these were simultaneous buy-bust operations in
Siquijor and Boracay Island targeting an organized group that defaced, mutilated, and
destroyed legal tender 10-piso coins, converting their inner cores into jewelry sold to tourists.
As part of its currency advocacy initiatives, the BSP expanded the Piso Caravan Project
in 2025. The number of participating partners nearly doubled, rising by 88.3 percent
to 2,326. The value of unfit and mutilated banknotes and coins exchanged reached
₱1.3 billion, marking a substantial increase from the previous year.
56 This is equivalent to 10.0 ppm, a decrease from 13.2 ppm in 2024.
52 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

What we did in 2025: Operational highlights
Research
The BSP recognizes that high-quality research underpins sound decision-making
and, thus, continues to strengthen its research function to support evidence-based
and forward-looking policies. In 2025, the central bank advanced a more rigorous,
applied research agenda, ensuring alignment between its analytical work and
policy priorities.
Research tools and methods were enhanced by disseminating research outputs,
deepening engagement and knowledge exchange, sustaining capacity-building
initiatives, and raising governance and quality standards. These efforts kept policies
grounded in stringent analysis, responsive to emerging risks, and consistent with
international best practices—delivering clear benefits for the Filipino people.
Advancing a policy-centered research function
Improving the quality of analysis is a continuing priority at the BSP. Efforts during
the year focused on building research capacity, refining internal processes, and
integrating research more effectively into policymaking. The central bank also
established dedicated research units and expanded collaboration with local and
international experts.
Coherence across studies was achieved through closer coordination among units,
tighter quality standards, and better alignment with the BSP’s policy agenda.
Greater emphasis was placed on research approaches that reflect domestic
conditions while maintaining internationally accepted methods.
BSP research for the period covered monetary policy transmission, inflation
dynamics and expectations, external developments, and financial markets,
alongside the use of advanced econometric and AI tools. Core policy concepts—
such as the neutral real interest rate, output gap, exchange rate pass-through, and
the non-accelerating inflation rate of unemployment—were also revisited and refined.
Integration into policy processes was further improved through regular
engagement between researchers and decision-makers. Updates provided to
senior management ensured that analytical work reflected operational priorities
and emerging issues.
Together, these initiatives fostered a more structured research environment,
combining specialized work across units with shared standards, coordination, and
continuous feedback.
54 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Expanding research, engagement, and thought leadership
The BSP maintains a strong research pace, reflecting the scope of its policy work
and the growing depth of its analysis. Several works were published in international
journals indexed in major citation databases, supporting efforts to improve research
quality and broaden global reach.
In 2025, 20 studies were released under the BSP Discussion Paper Series.
BSP researchers also published 13 articles in peer-reviewed journals, covering
monetary policy, inflation, international economics, and related topics.
Beyond publications, BSP research was presented at major conferences and
academic forums across the US, Europe, and Asia. These engagements provided
opportunities for feedback, sharpening analysis and linking BSP work to global
discourse.
The central bank also actively convened dialogues on key policy issues and
emerging economic challenges. In January 2025, the BSP and the BIS co-organized
the Workshop on Macroeconomic Models, focusing on macro-financial policy
frameworks and advances in macroeconomic modeling.
In July 2025, the BSP hosted the 5th International Research Fair themed
“From Pandemic and High Inflation to Global Fragmentation and Structural
Transformation: New Central Banking Challenges.” The fair brought together
central banks, multilateral institutions, and the academe to discuss evolving
macroeconomic risks and policy responses. The inaugural Central Banking
Symposium followed in November 2025.
Internationally, the BSP co-organized a poster session at the Central Bank
Research Association Annual Meeting. Held at Harvard Business School in
August 2025, it served as a venue for BSP research staff to engage with the global
academic community. The Bank also co-organized the 20th East Asian Economic
Association Conference. A semi-plenary session, “The Mariano Legacy:
Econometrics, Policy, and Institution-Building in East Asia,” honored Filipino
economist Roberto S. Mariano for his contributions to the development of the
BSP’s macroeconometric models.
Locally, the BSP co-sponsored the Philippine Institute for Development Studies
Annual Public Policy Conference in September 2025. With the theme
“Reimagining Governance in the Age of AI,” it highlighted the role of digital
technologies in shaping public policy and the continued support for policy
research in the country. In November 2025, the Bank contributed to the
63rd Philippine Economic Society Annual Meeting themed “An Economy that
Works for People: Sustaining Economic Progress Amidst Global Polycrisis.”
BSP-led sessions covered inflation dynamics, exchange rate pass-through, and
machine learning-based forecasting.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 55

Strengthening analytical capacity through strategic collaboration
Strategic partnerships and expert engagement are key drivers in strengthening
analytical capacity. Accordingly, the BSP expanded its collaboration with the BIS,
including through the Asian Consultative Council Research Network, and with
the South East Asian Central Banks (SEACEN) Research and Training Centre.
The Bank engaged experts from the University of Pennsylvania and the Asia School
of Business to deepen the use of advanced econometric and machine learning
techniques for nowcasting, forecasting, and systemic risk analysis.
The BSP also leveraged its Research Experts Panel (REP), with Francis E. Warnock
joining in 2025. REP engagements provided technical input across various research
areas and improved analytical rigor and international comparability.
Internal platforms, such as the Research Huddle Series, supported early-stage work
through peer review and technical discussion. Around 40 studies were presented
during the year, including sessions with experts from Trinity College Dublin,
the SEACEN Research and Training Centre, and the Hong Kong University of Science
and Technology.
At the same time, the BSP initiated a review of its monetary policy framework with
internationally recognized experts Athanasios Orphanides and Stephen Cecchetti.
The review aims to enhance forward-looking policy analysis and align institutional
practices with global standards, reinforcing the role of rigorous, policy-relevant
research.
Enhancing financial stability analysis, surveillance, and outreach
The BSP combines reporting with research-based knowledge sharing to raise
awareness of systemic risks and support informed decision-making. In 2025,
central bank research informed policy discussions and deepened understanding of
emerging vulnerabilities.
Findings were presented at major conferences, including the 2025 Annual Meeting
of the Central Bank Research Association, the 20th International Convention of the
East Asian Economic Association, and the 16th National Convention on Statistics.
Collaboration with academic experts focused on interconnectedness, stress
testing, and dynamic stochastic general equilibrium modeling.
Another key initiative was the closing conference for the BSP book project on
financial crises: Risk and Resilience in the Philippine Financial System: How Much
Has Changed?. The event provided a platform for authors to present findings and
updates and helped broaden stakeholder engagement.
Following its study visit to the Bank of England in 2024, the BSP pursued technical
assistance to strengthen communication on systemic risk. This engagement
aimed to improve the Financial Stability Report, particularly its structure, content,
and messaging, emphasizing the need for clearer, more transparent articulation of
risk assessments and macroprudential policy initiatives.
56 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

The BSP also worked with local and foreign stakeholders to enhance systemic
risk surveillance. Market engagement, including discussions with real estate
stakeholders, complemented data analysis with private sector insights. A study visit
to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York covered its vulnerabilities framework,
collateral pre-positioning for emergency lending, stress testing design, and
financial stability policy communication.
In collaboration with the FSCC, the BSP adopted efforts to close data gaps on NBFIs
and NFCs. Initial work focused on non-bank financial intermediaries and will extend
to other NBFI categories, including institutional investors and FMIs.
Efforts were also strengthened to deepen the analysis of NFC-related vulnerabilities,
particularly the potential systemic effects of complex conglomerates. Undertaken
alongside the FSCC and other regulators such as the Securities and Exchange
Commission (SEC) and the Insurance Commission (IC), this initiative promotes
enhanced surveillance and evidence-based policymaking by improving
understanding of linkages across banks, NBFIs, and NFCs.
In parallel, the BSP pursued research in support of the country’s capital market.
These included thematic studies on corporate debt characteristics to inform
corporate bond market development. The FSCC remained a venue for discussing
related initiatives, leveraging its membership.
Fostering an integrated and forward-looking research agenda
These developments point to a more cohesive and aligned research function,
integrating decentralized work with stronger coordination, clearer standards, and
tighter links to policy.
At its core is a simple principle: Policy decisions are only as sound as the analysis
behind them. Continued investment in research capacity—including advanced
platforms, robust publication standards, and greater visibility in international
research networks—remains central to the BSP’s strategy.
Looking ahead, the BSP will further enhance its research capabilities to keep policy
frameworks adaptive, credible, and grounded. The Bank will also take a more active
role in regional policy dialogue and research collaboration within the ASEAN,
particularly on issues related to monetary policy transmission, financial stability,
and structural transformation.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 57

Sustainability agenda
The BSP continues to advance its sustainability agenda across three reinforcing
fronts: (a) strengthening climate and environmental risk analytics and supervision,
(b) mobilizing sustainable finance markets, and (c) embedding sustainability in its
governance and operations. Together, these efforts reflect the Bank’s commitment
to building a climate-resilient financial system that supports inclusive adaptation
and an orderly transition.
Managing climate and environmental risks
Sound policy and effective supervision require strong climate risk analytics
and prudential frameworks. In 2025, the BSP further integrated climate and
environmental risks into financial supervision and policymaking, including
enhancements to the regulatory relief framework for banks in calamity-affected
areas, to support operational resilience. These efforts—focused on strengthening
supervisory frameworks and tools, enhancing data and modeling capabilities,
and building institutional expertise—equip supervisors and policymakers
with more robust and relevant mechanisms to assess and manage climate and
environmental risks.
Advancing climate risk stress testing
Climate risk stress testing (CRST) remains a core component of the BSP’s
supervisory approach. It enables the assessment of banks’ resilience under severe
yet plausible climate scenarios. As an evolving discipline, CRST requires specialized
methods and granular data. To this end, the BSP continues to work with
development partners and the banking sector to strengthen supervisory and
bank-level capabilities.
In partnership with the WB, the BSP conducted CRST workshops on 31 March and
02 April 2025. The workshops enhanced participants’ understanding of how
physical and transition risks can amplify traditional banking risks—such as credit,
market, operational, and liquidity risks—and introduced alternative stress testing
approaches and data requirements. These help banks proactively identify and
manage climate- and environment-related financial exposures.
The BSP also worked with BSFIs to integrate climate risks into the stress testing
framework. Key priorities include: (a) developing supervisory guidelines to promote
consistency and transparency in scenario design, (b) identifying transmission
channels affecting banks’ risk profiles and portfolios, and (c) enhancing reporting
requirements to improve the availability of granular, decision-useful data for climate
risk assessment.
58 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Reinforcing operational resilience for climate-related calamity events
Climate-related and other calamity events continue to put operational and
financial pressures on financial institutions. In response, the BSP enhanced its
regulatory relief framework to help banks sustain the delivery of critical financial
services, facilitate recovery and rehabilitation, and manage elevated credit and
operational risks. These improvements reinforce banks’ resilience, safeguard
financial system stability, and support borrowers affected by calamities.
Building an integrated climate data and analytics platform
Effective climate analytics depend on reliable, structured, and accessible data.
However, such data are often fragmented across government databases, private
sector sources, international organizations, and research repositories. To address
this, the BSP developed the Sustainability Platform for Analytics, Research, and
Knowledge (SPARK)—an integrated system for collecting, processing, analyzing,
and visualizing climate-related data leveraging AI capabilities.
SPARK provides BSP staff with a centralized and standardized data hub.
By consolidating datasets and improving data integrity, the platform enables more
efficient climate-related research and supervisory work. Further enhancements
are underway to expand access to timely, decision-ready climate information.
Deepening macroeconomic and financial system modeling
The BSP is developing macroeconomic and analytical frameworks that incorporate
climate considerations into its modeling work. Central to this effort is the Dynamic
Ecosystem-Finance-Economy (DEFINE) model, which captures interactions among
the economy, the financial system, and environmental variables. Once operational,
DEFINE is intended to support analysis of how climate shocks and transition
pathways may affect output, inflation, sectoral performance, and financial stability.
In addition, the BSP leverages third-party resources to assess the macroeconomic
impacts of climate scenarios. These include the Oxford Economics Global Climate
Service, which provides scenario-based, country-level projections that quantify
macroeconomic and sectoral effects of climate risks, transition pathways, and
policy responses.
Developing internal capability on climate risk analysis
Institutional expertise is critical to translating climate policy into action. As such,
the BSP continually deepens its climate analytics capabilities across research,
economic policy, and supervisory functions—ensuring climate considerations are
systematically embedded into macro-financial analysis.
In 2025, the BSP conducted targeted capacity-building initiatives. Among these are
the Introduction to Geographic Information System and Remote Sensing workshop,
delivered by experts from the Philippine Space Agency, on 28–30 April 2025, and
the Briefing on the 2024 Philippine Climate Change Assessment on 26 May 2025.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 59

BSP staff also participated in various training programs organized by the SEACEN
Research and Training Centre, the Asian Development Bank Institute (ADBI), and
the Network for Greening the Financial System to stay aligned with international
standards and emerging methodologies in climate research and modeling.
These included the Regulation and Supervision of Climate Risk and Disclosure
Standards Course on 5–8 May 2025, the Data and Vulnerability Analysis Online
Course on 1–3 September 2025, and the Taxonomies and Scaling up Sustainable
Finance Course on 7–9 October 2025.
Mobilizing green finance
Mobilizing finance for climate action remains essential to building resilience,
supporting adaptation, and enabling a low-carbon transition. In 2025, the BSP
focused on turning policy frameworks into practice through taxonomy
implementation support, industry capacity building, and structured stakeholder
engagement. These efforts help reduce uncertainty, improve market readiness,
and channel capital toward climate-aligned activities.
Operationalizing the sustainable finance taxonomy guidelines
To promote consistent application of the Philippine Sustainable Finance Taxonomy
Guidelines (SFTG), the BSP issued additional clarificatory guidance in 2025.
This guidance focused on the use of the taxonomy in product development and
capital allocation.
In July 2025, the BSP released another series of frequently asked questions (FAQs)
on the SFTG. This clarified the taxonomy’s scope, coverage, economic activity
assessment, and interaction with existing regulatory incentives, supporting more
consistent implementation across banks.
The BSP also issued a non-exhaustive reference list of climate adaptation-related
projects and potential activities aligned with priority sectors under the National
Adaptation Plan (NAP) in October 2025.57 This reference assists banks in designing
adaptation-focused financial products while reinforcing the need to assess
activities against the taxonomy’s criteria on “substantial contribution” and “do no
significant harm.”
Strengthening regulatory incentives for green and sustainable lending
In December 2025, the MB approved a two-year extension of the regulatory incentive
for financing eligible green or sustainable projects, effective 06 January 2026.58
Initially introduced in 2023,59 the incentive allows banks to exceed the single
borrower’s limit of 25.0 percent by up to an additional 15.0 percent for lending to
sustainable projects.
57 BSP Memorandum No. M-2025-033 (Philippine Sustainable Finance Taxonomy Guidelines: Sample Climate
Adaptation-related Projects and Activities)
58 BSP Circular No. 1227 dated 05 January 2026 (Extension of Regulatory Incentive Period for Financing for Eligible
Green or Sustainable Projects and Activities)
59 BSP Circular No. 1185 dated 13 December 2023 (Grant of Additional Single Borrower’s Limit for Financing Eligible
Projects and Zero percent Reserve Requirement Rate Against Sustainable Bonds)
60 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Banks may also use all proceeds from sustainable bond issuances to finance
eligible activities. These proceeds are exempt from the usual reserve requirements
applied to bank-issued bonds.
Providing regulatory incentives supports sustained financing for renewable
energy, water and wastewater systems, clean transportation, and climate-resilient
infrastructure. It also supports other eligible activities aligned with the Philippines’
nationally determined contributions and the NAP.
Mobilizing partners for green finance
Channeling capital toward the country’s adaptation and transition priorities cannot
rely on regulation alone. In 2025, the BSP broadened its engagement with the
financial industry, development partners, and international organizations to build
coalitions and shared knowledge to scale sustainable finance beyond early adopters.
From 15 to 16 October 2025, the BSP co-hosted the Blended Finance Forum for
the Philippines with Convergence and Australia’s Department of Foreign Affairs
and Trade. The forum facilitated discussions on building a pipeline of investable
adaptation projects, mobilizing domestic capital, and applying blended finance
structures in priority sectors such as agriculture; micro, small, and medium
enterprises (MSMEs); and nature-based solutions.
The BSP also partnered with the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and
Development (OECD) through its Clean Energy Finance and Investment Mobilisation
Program to implement the project Enhancing Access to Clean Energy Finance
for Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises in the Philippines. The initiative aims
to expand financing options and strengthen enabling regulations for MSME
investments in clean energy. A workshop marked the launch of the project on
06 November 2025.
Moreover, the BSP convened forums under its Knowledge Exchange Series on
Sustainability. These included the Climate Risk Modeling for Adaptation and
Resilience Webinar, in partnership with the United Nations (UN) Environment
Programme Finance Initiative’s V20 Sustainable Insurance Facility, and the
Roundtable Discussion on Innovative Financing for a Renewable Philippines,
in collaboration with the Department of Energy (DOE) and Bloomberg New
Energy Finance.
Implementing sustainability in central banking operations
Sustainability at the BSP extends beyond its regulatory and supervisory mandates
to how it manages resources, conducts operations, and measures environmental
impact. In 2025, the BSP strengthened this internal agenda by embedding ESG
considerations into its investment decisions, promoting responsible workplace
practices and innovation, and enhancing the measurement and disclosure of its
greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.
2025 BSP Annual Report | 61

Embedding sustainability in reserves management
The BSP advanced its responsible investing approach by formalizing its Responsible
Investment Policy in April 2025. This policy integrates ESG considerations across
investment processes, balancing financial prudence with positive environmental
and social outcomes through ESG integration, screening, impact investing, and
collaboration. As a signatory to the UN Principles for Responsible Investment,
the BSP also submitted its first mandatory report in August 2025, affirming its
commitment to transparency and sustainable finance.
Beyond domestic policy reforms, the BSP contributed to global efforts on
sustainable investment innovation by participating in an advisory group of central
banks and the BIS. The advisory group guided the development of a climate-aware
corporate bond fund.
Greening currency production processes
Sustainability is increasingly embedded in the BSP’s currency production.
The transition to a cyanide-free plating process is underway, marking a significant
step toward safer and more environmentally responsible production of
commemorative coins, medals, and state decorations. This initiative is backed
by a technology licensing agreement with the Department of Science and
Technology (DOST) Metals Industry Research and Development Center, ensuring
alignment with sound and sustainable innovation practices.
In parallel, the BSP enhanced its effluent treatment system for banknote printing,
designed to recycle and reuse portions of banknote processing solutions.
An additional decanter—used to separate solids from liquid waste—was installed.
This improves wastewater treatment efficiency and reduces the need for additional
chemical inputs, lowering the environmental footprint of currency production.
Cultivating a culture of sustainability
The BSP strengthened efforts to cultivate a culture where sustainability is reflected
not only in operational practices but also in individual responsibility. Recognizing
that meaningful environmental stewardship requires both institutional reforms
and behavioral change, the BSP advanced initiatives encouraging employees to
integrate sustainability into their daily work and decisions.
In July 2025, the BSP launched its Toward Zero Waste Program. The program
establishes a consistent and holistic approach to waste management across
operations, procurement, infrastructure, and employee awareness. By translating
sustainability principles into daily actions, it strengthens the link between
individual behavior and the institution’s overall environmental impact.
To reinforce leadership alignment, the BSP convened a leadership and sustainability
summit for its senior leaders and management team on 05 December 2025.
The summit brought together industry leaders, regulators, and sustainability
practitioners to deepen understanding of climate and environmental risks and
explore how central banking policies and operations can support a sustainable future.
62 | 2025 BSP Annual Report

Establishing a baseline for greenhouse gas emissions
The BSP continually enhances the environmental performance of its facilities
through sustained compliance with energy, water, and resource efficiency
standards. Building on its BERDE Certification obtained in 2024,60 efforts during
the year focused on improving emissions management, reinforcing the Bank’s
commitment to sustainable and climate-resilient operations.
Credible sustainability commitments require reliable measurement. In 2025,
the BSP conducted a comprehensive, Bank-wide data collection exercise covering
Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions, as well as selected Scope 3 categories.61 While the
current baseline does not yet include Scope 1 emissions from refrigerants,
nor key Scope 3 categories—such as employee commuting, business travel,
and procurement—it marks an important step toward institutionalizing GHG
measurement across BSP facilities. This effort improves visibility over energy use,
fuel consumption, and other material sources of environmental impact.
Results from this baseline assessment show that the BSP’s emissions profile
is largely driven by Scope 2 emissions (Table 4). This pattern reflects the electricity-
dependent nature of the Bank’s office-based and technology-enabled operations.

and reliability improve, the BSP will be better positioned to set evidence-based
emissions reduction targets and report progress with greater transparency.
